--- Page 1 ---
<meta>Title label from the cover of a traditional Chinese thread-bound volume of the Sancai Tuhui. This marks the beginning of a significant Ming Dynasty pictorial encyclopedia.</meta>

# The Collected Illustrations of the Three Realms

<note>original: 三才圖會 (*Sancai Tuhui*)</note>

<term>The Three Realms<note>The "Three Powers" or "Three Realms" (*Sancai*) refers to the traditional Chinese cosmological categorization of the universe into Heaven, Earth, and Humanity.</note></term>

<note>Compiled by the scholars Wang Qi and Wang Siyi and published around 1609, this 106-volume encyclopedia was designed to be a comprehensive visual and textual record of all known things, from astronomical phenomena to local geography and human biography.</note>

<summary>This is the title label for a monumental 17th-century Chinese encyclopedia that categorizes the world into the "Three Realms" of Heaven, Earth, and Humanity. It stands as one of the most important illustrated reference works of the Ming Dynasty.</summary>

<keywords>Sancai Tuhui, Ming Dynasty, Encyclopedia, Three Realms, Chinese cosmology, Wang Qi, woodblock printing</keywords>

It follows the general title page and outlines the specific visual and literary diagrams included in this volume, focusing on historical charts, poetic puzzles, and famous inscriptions.</meta>

<note>This table of contents highlights the Ming Dynasty’s fascination with "Literary History" (文史), which combined historical record-keeping with the appreciation of decorative inscriptions and "palindromes" (迴文)—poems written in circular or grid patterns that could be read in various directions to reveal different meanings.</note>

<note>The mention of "Bai Letian" refers to Bai Juyi (772–846), one of the most popular Tang Dynasty poets, whose visit to the "Purple Cloud Palace" represents a common theme of scholar-officials engaging with Daoist or mystical landscapes.</note>

<summary>This page is a Table of Contents for the third volume of the Literary History section, listing maps of ancient Chinese states alongside diagrams of famous inscriptions and complex poetic word games. It illustrates the intersection of history, calligraphy, and literary ingenuity in Ming Dynasty scholarship.</summary>

<keywords>Table of Contents, Spring and Autumn Period, Palindromes, Tang Yin, Fan Zhongyan, Bai Juyi, Inscriptions, Literary History, Sancai Tuhui</keywords>

# Illustrated Guide to Lyric Poetry
<note>original: 詩餘圖譜; The term "Shiyu" (literally "remnants of poetry") refers to the *Ci* genre—lyrics written to existing musical tunes. This section likely provides visual templates for various rhythmic patterns.</note>

<column-break/>

# Diagram of the Palindrome using Shared Characters
<note>original: 借字迴文圖; A "shared character" palindrome is a sophisticated word puzzle where specific characters in a circular or grid layout are "borrowed" or read twice to complete the poetic meter when reading in different directions.</note>

<term>Spring and Autumn (春秋)</term>: A period in Chinese history (771–476 BCE) named after the *Spring and Autumn Annals*, a chronicle of the State of Lu.
<term>Chronological Table (年表)</term>: A grid-style layout used by Chinese historians to compare events happening simultaneously across different regions or states.
<term>King Ping (平王)</term>: The ruler who moved the Zhou capital east to Luoyang, beginning the Eastern Zhou period.
<term>Duke Yin (隱公)</term>: The first ruler of the State of Lu recorded in the *Spring and Autumn Annals*.

<summary>This page presents a synoptic chronological table correlating the reigns of the Zhou Kings (Ping and Huan) with the rulers of twenty different feudal states during the early Spring and Autumn Period. It uses dense interlinear notes to record specific genealogical links and diplomatic events between these competing powers.</summary>
<keywords>Spring and Autumn Period, chronology, King Ping of Zhou, Duke Yin of Lu, feudal states, Chinese historiography, Eastern Zhou Dynasty</keywords>

<note>
This table uses the reign of Duke Xuan of Lu (r. 608–591 BCE) as a temporal anchor to track events across the fractured landscape of ancient China. The dates are further specified using the "sexagenary cycle" (e.g., *Gui-Chou*, *Jia-Yin*), a traditional 60-year rotating calendar system. 

Note that two different states are mentioned here that are both pronounced "Qi" in modern Mandarin: the powerful State of Qi (齊) in the first column and the smaller State of Qi (杞, sometimes called Ba) in the final column.
</note>

<summary>This page records a decade of political transitions (601–593 BCE) during the Spring and Autumn period, documenting the deaths and successions of rulers in the states of Lu, Qi, Ju, and the minor state of Qi (Ba).</summary>

<note>
This table serves as a "timeline of chaos" for the State of Jin during the early Spring and Autumn period. The primary focus is the **Quwo-Jin War**, a decades-long struggle where a branch of the ruling family based in Quwo eventually usurped the main throne.

Key figures mentioned:
1. **Duke Min of Jin** (緡公): The last ruler of the elder line of Jin.
2. **Duke Wu of Quwo** (曲沃武公): The victor who eventually became Duke Wu of Jin.
3. **King Xi and King Hui**: Monarchs of the Eastern Zhou Dynasty, used here to provide a central imperial date for regional events.

The use of the sexagenary cycle (Bing-Xu, Ding-Hai, etc.) allows historians to pin these events to approximately 679–671 BCE.
</note>

<summary>This page catalogs the political instability and succession struggles in the State of Jin during the mid-7th century BCE, synchronizing local events with the reigns of the Zhou Kings.</summary>
<keywords>State of Jin, Quwo, Duke Min, Duke Wu, King Hui of Zhou, King Xi of Zhou, Spring and Autumn Period, chronology</keywords>

# Chronological Table of the Twelve Feudal Princes

## The State of Lu <margin>The State of Lu</margin>

<note>The top row of the table tracks the years of King Hui of the Zhou Dynasty (reigned 676–652 BCE), who served as the nominal sovereign over all the feudal states. The second row tracks the specific reigns of the Dukes of Lu. This period was marked by intense internal strife within the Lu ducal family, leading to the assassinations and rapid successions noted here.</note>

# Chart of the Successions of the State of Lu

<margin>
The *Gongyang Commentary* <note>One of the three classic commentaries on the *Spring and Autumn Annals*, focused on the "praise and blame" philosophy of history.</note> states that in Lu, there was "one birth and one reaching." The annotation explains: "A father dying and being succeeded by his son is called *birth* <note>original: 生 (sheng); referring to direct generational succession</note>; brothers succeeding one another is called *reaching* <note>original: 及 (ji); referring to fraternal succession</note>."
</margin>

<meta>This page transitions from the genealogical records of the State of Lu to a famous literary and biographical account: the story of the poetess Su Hui and the origin of her "Xuanji Tu" (Palindrome Map). # Preface to the Palindrome Map of the Turning Spheres
<note>original: 璇璣圖 (Xuanji Tu). This refers to a famous 841-character literary puzzle where a poem is woven into a grid that can be read in thousands of directions—forward, backward, horizontally, vertically, and diagonally.</note>

During the time of Fu Jian <note>The third emperor of the Former Qin dynasty, reigning from 357–385 CE.</note> of the Former Qin, the Inspector of Qinzhou was Dou Tao of Fufeng. His wife, Lady Su, was the third daughter of Su Daozhi, the Magistrate of Wugong in Chenliu. Her given name was Hui, and her courtesy name <note>A name given at adulthood to be used by peers and elders instead of one's birth name.</note> was Lanzhi. 

She was exceptionally intelligent, possessed a refined and beautiful appearance, and was known for her humble and quiet self-discipline, never seeking fame or recognition. At the age of sixteen, she was married into the Dou family. Tao held her in great respect. However, Lady Su’s nature was somewhat prone to impulsiveness, which occasionally manifested as a wounded sense of jealousy.

Tao, whose courtesy name was Lianbo, was the grandson of the Senior General Qianzhen and the second son of Lang. He possessed a majestic and elegant presence and was deeply learned in both the Confucian Classics and historical texts. He was praised by his contemporaries as being "balanced in both the civil and military arts" <note>original: 允文允武 (yunwen yunwu); a classical ideal of a scholar-warrior.</note>. 

Fu Jian entrusted him with responsibilities as a confidant and a "heart and spine" <note>original: 心膂 (xinlü); a term for a most trusted and essential official.</note> of the state. He served in a succession of prominent positions, in all of which he gained a reputation for excellent governance. He was later promoted to Inspector of Qinzhou, but because he defied an imperial decree, he was exiled to the frontier at Dunhuang.

When Fu Jian conquered Xiangyang <note>A strategically vital city in modern Hubei province.</note> from the Jin Dynasty, he feared the threat of further conflict. Relying on Tao’s talent and strategic brilliance, he issued an edict appointing him General of the South and stationed him to guard Xiangyang. Previously, Tao had a favorite concubine named Zhao Yangtai, whose excellence in singing and dancing was unsurpassed. Tao kept her in a separate residence...

<summary>This page introduces the famous 4th-century poetess Su Hui and her husband Dou Tao, detailing their characters and the political career that eventually led to their separation. It sets the historical stage for the creation of her famous "Xuanji Tu" palindrome poem, which she wove to win back her husband's affection.</summary>

<keywords>Su Hui, Dou Tao, Xuanji Tu, Palindrome Map, Former Qin Dynasty, Women's Literature, Fu Jian, Xiangyang</keywords>

--- Page 38 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Lady Su (Su Hui) from the previous page, detailing the conflict between her and her husband, Dou Tao, which led to the creation of the famous Palindrome Map.</meta>

### Collected Illustrations of the Three Realms: Literature and History, Volume 3

...When Lady Su found out about her <note>Referring to Dou Tao’s favorite concubine, Yangtai, mentioned on the previous page.</note>, she sought her out and captured her, subjecting her to harsh whippings and humiliation. Tao felt deep resentment over this treatment of his concubine. For her part, Yangtai took every opportunity to watch for Lady Su’s shortcomings, constantly bringing forth deceitful slanders. Tao became increasingly incensed with Lady Su, who was twenty-one years old at the time.

When Tao was ordered to command the defense of Xiangyang, he invited Lady Su to accompany him. Still harboring resentment, Lady Su refused to travel with him. Consequently, Tao took Yangtai with him to his post and severed all communication with Lady Su.

Lady Su was filled with regret and sorrow. In her solitude, she wove a piece of silk brocade into a **palindrome** <term>回文 (huiwen); a text that can be read in multiple directions while remaining coherent.</term>. Five colors illuminated one another, dazzling the heart and shining in the eyes. It was eight inches square and featured over two hundred poems, totaling more than eight hundred words. Whether read vertically, horizontally, backward, or forward, they all formed literary compositions. In these texts, every stroke and dot was without flaw. The brilliance of her talent and emotion surpassed the present and transcended the past. It was named the **Map of the Turning Spheres** <note>original: 璇璣圖 (Xuanji Tu). The name refers to the rotating mechanism of an armillary sphere, representing the way the poems "rotate" and change meaning depending on the direction they are read.</note>.

However, readers were unable to fully comprehend its complexities. Lady Su smiled and said, "It turns and winds, lingering and revolving; it is its own language. Unless one is of my family, none shall be able to decipher it."

She then dispatched a servant <term>蒼頭 (cangtou); literally "blue head," a term for a domestic servant or slave based on the color of their headcloth.</term> to carry the brocade to Xiangyang. When Tao looked upon it, he was deeply moved by its exquisite perfection. Consequently, he sent Yangtai away to the capital <note>original: 闕中 (quezhong); the area within the imperial palace gates, here used to mean the capital city or a secondary residence.</note> and prepared carriages and attendants. With grand ceremony, he invited Lady Su to return to the region south of the Han River.

<summary>This page recounts how Lady Su, after being separated from her husband due to a domestic dispute, wove the intricate "Xuanji Tu" palindrome to express her feelings, leading to their reconciliation.</summary>
<keywords>Su Hui, Dou Tao, Yangtai, Xuanji Tu, Palindrome, Xiangyang, Silk Brocade, Former Qin Dynasty</keywords>

->Benevolence and wisdom cherish virtue, like the sagely emperors Yu and Tang; true wonders reveal glory in repeating crimson chapters. Worthy ministers and the holy empress match the heroic sovereign; a couple separated, drifting and floating upon the river’s crossing.<-

**Technical Vocabulary:**
*   **Map of the Turning Spheres:** The title of the palindrome poem (*Xuanji Tu*).
*   **Su Hui:** The poet and weaver, also known as Lady Su.
*   **Palindrome Poem:** A poem that can be read in multiple directions (*Huiwen shi*).
*   **Empress Wu Zetian:** The only female emperor in Chinese history, who wrote a famous preface praising this work.
*   **Woven Brocade:** The physical medium of the poem, created on a loom (*Zhijin*).

In the pure temple, the phoenix flies. Long sorrow is felt in all directions. The husband is sincere, yet the old is discarded for the new. The gentleman thinks only of the new, while the chaste wife watches the clouds of spring.

The fragrant orchid withers in the bright spring sun. Facing the wall with a different intent, I am confused by the old and the new. One must be pure and harmonious in will, waiting for the one they love.

# Pride and Excess

->Read horizontally from the four sides to form five-character verses<-

In the cold season, one recognizes the withered pine <note>The pine tree is a traditional Chinese symbol of longevity and moral integrity because it stays green in winter</note>; the true nature of things is known from beginning to end. As beauty fades and appearance changes, the benevolent and the worthy are distinguished by their conduct. <gloss>Read in reverse to also form a verse</gloss>

<gloss>The verse formed:</gloss> The conduct of a gentleman distinguishes the worthy and benevolent; as beauty and appearance change through decline and decay, the beginning and end reveal the truth of things; the withered pine is recognized in the cold of the year.

->Crossing patterns form verses; all are in four-character lines<-

Slanderers and fawning sycophants, treacherous and wicked, harm my loyalty and chastity. The cause of disaster lies in what one relies upon; it breeds extreme pride and excess. <gloss>Read in reverse</gloss>

Using Colors to Distinguish Sections <gloss>Only one corner is presented here; the rest follow this example</gloss>

Horizontal application of color

<term>Pride and Excess (jiāo yíng)</term>: A classical concept warning that arrogance and over-abundance lead inevitably to a fall.
<term>Palindrome (huí wén)</term>: A literary form where the text can be read forward, backward, or in various directions to create new meanings.
<term>Five-character (wǔ yán)</term>: A standard meter in Chinese poetry consisting of five syllables per line.
<term>Four-character (sì yán)</term>: An ancient poetic meter, often used in the *Classic of Poetry* and formal compositions.
<term>Cold season (hán suì)</term>: A metaphor for times of hardship or political danger.
<term>Withered pine (diāo sōng)</term>: Specifically refers to the pine's ability to endure the frost while other plants perish.
<term>Slanderers and sycophants (chán nìng)</term>: Corrupt court officials or individuals who use false praise to manipulate others.
<term>Loyalty and chastity (zhōng zhēn)</term>: The core virtues Su Hui asserts in the face of her husband’s desertion.
<term>Cause of disaster (huò yīn)</term>: The root reason for a misfortune.

# Collected Illustrations of the Three Realms

The winds of Zhou <note>original: "周風" (Zhōu fēng). Refers to the "Airs of Zhou" from the *Classic of Poetry*, symbolizing the moral influence of virtuous women</note> arose from the Queens; Lady Fan of Chu <note>Lady Fan was a consort of King Zhuang of Chu, famous for her integrity and for rebuking the king's excesses</note> maintained her integrity within the inner chambers. <gloss>The characters "Zhou" and "Chu" use yellow; the other ten characters use green</gloss>

Long constrained and unable to spread wings and fly; a pair of birds begins to sing, and my sorrow is immense. <gloss>The characters "Long" and "Pair" use yellow; the other ten characters use pink</gloss>

For whom is this flowery, seductive appearance? The notes of the palace and feather <note>The "Palace" (Gong) and "Feather" (Yu) are two of the five notes in the traditional Chinese pentatonic scale</note> follow one another in harmony. <gloss>The characters "Flower" and "Palace" use yellow; the other ten characters use blue</gloss>

---

->The above are read in sequence according to this pattern to form a complete composition<-

---

The inner gardens are in disarray...

It is difficult for the bright to endure... <gloss>Use green</gloss>

Glory, if it is not through righteousness...

### Another Method for Reading the Five Colors <gloss>see the *Wugong County Gazette*</gloss> <note>The *Wugong County Gazette* is a local historical record from the region where the poetess Su Hui lived; it is a primary source for the preservation of this poem's complex decoding methods.</note>

# The "Poem on a Plate" by the Wife of Su Boyu

<image-desc>A circular diagram containing Traditional Chinese characters arranged in a complex spiral pattern, known as a "Poem on a Plate" (Pan Zhong Shi) or a "Palindromic Poem" (Hui Wen Shi). The text begins at the center character "Mountain" (山) and winds outwards in concentric paths toward the edge. Below the circle, a line of text provides instructions on how to read the poem.</image-desc>

->[One] should begin from the center and circle out toward the four corners<-
<note>original: 當從中央周四角. This instruction serves as the key to "unlocking" the poem, guiding the reader's eye through the spiral to maintain the correct meter and rhyme.</note>

**Su Boyu** <term>Su Boyu (蘇伯玉): A historical figure of the Western Jin dynasty (266–316 AD). He was stationed away from home in the remote region of Shu, prompting his wife to send him this poem as a token of her devotion.</term>, **Poem on a Plate** <term>Pan Zhong Shi (盤中詩): A famous example of visual poetry where the text is written in a spiral on the surface of a plate. It is intended to be read by rotating the vessel.</term>, **Palindromic Poem** <term>Hui Wen Shi (迴文詩): A sophisticated literary form in which the text can be read in multiple directions—forward, backward, or in a cycle—while remaining grammatically and poetically coherent.</term>

<summary>This page introduces the "Poem on a Plate" composed by Su Boyu's wife, a circular piece of visual literature designed to be read in a spiral from the center outward. It provides the essential decoding instruction and defines the historical context of the poem as a message of longing sent to a distant husband.</summary>

<keywords>Su Boyu, Poem on a Plate, Pan Zhong Shi, Palindromic poetry, visual literature, Western Jin dynasty, spiral reading</keywords>

<meta>This page provides the full text of the "Poem on a Plate" (Pan Zhong Shi) introduced on the previous page. It transitions from the visual description of the spiral diagram to the actual literary content, which expresses the heartache of a wife left behind in Chang'an while her husband, Su Boyu, serves in the remote region of Shu.</meta>

# Collected Illustrations of the Three Realms: Literature and History, Volume 3
## Page 18

Mountain trees are high, birds sing mournfully.
Spring waters are deep, carp are fat.
Sparrows in an empty granary often suffer from hunger;
<note>The "empty granary" serves as a metaphor for the wife’s own neglected household and her emotional "starvation" during her husband's long absence.</note>
The wife of an official rarely meets her husband.
I go out the door to gaze and see a white robe <note>original: 白衣 (baiyi). In this period, white was the color of undyed, common clothing often worn by travelers.</note>—
I thought it was you, but it was someone else.
I return through the door, my heart filled with grief.
I go north into the hall, west onto the stairs;
The loom creaks harshly, the shuttle's sound hurries me.
A long sigh—to whom should I speak?
You are on a journey, and I long for you.
There was a day you left, but no date for your return.
I tie my sash in a knot of eternal longing.
If you forget me, I cannot know;
If I were to forget you, I should be punished.
I have my path of virtue, which you ought to know.
Gold is yellow, jade is white;
Mountains are high, and valleys are low.
<note>These lines emphasize the natural and "correct" order of the world, suggesting her devotion is as fundamental as the color of gold or the height of a mountain.</note>
His surname is Su, his style name is Boyu.
His talent is great, his wisdom and schemes are plenty.
His home is in Chang'an, but his body is in Shu.
Why be so stingy with horse-hooves that you do not return often?
A thousand pounds of mutton, a hundred measures of wine—
I would fatten your horse with wheat and grain.
<note>She promises a lavish homecoming celebration, offering to care for his horse just to ensure he has no excuse to stay away.</note>
People today know "four feet" <term>Four feet (四足): A pun referring to both the four-legged horses the husband rides and the "four corners" of the physical plate the poem is written on.</term>,
Yet they cannot read this letter.

->One should begin from the center and circle out toward the four corners.<-

These diagrams, known as "circular inscriptions" (panming), were designed to be read while rotating the vessel, blending moral instruction with the physical act of drinking.</meta>

# Yin Zhongkan
<note>Yin Zhongkan (died c. 399 AD) was a prominent governor and intellectual of the Jin Dynasty. He was famously known for his literary talent and his devotion to his father, though he met a tragic end during the power struggles of the era.</note>

# Inscription on a Wine Plate
<note>A "Jiu Pan Ming" is a short, rhythmic text inscribed on a shallow drinking vessel or tray. It served as a "memento mori" or a guide to conduct, reminding the drinker of virtue even in moments of leisure.</note>

->**The First Diagram: The Substance of Conduct**<-

| **Action** | **Direction** | **Instruction** |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Top** | North | Propriety |
| **Top-Right** | Northeast | Moderation |
| **Right** | East | Possession |
| **Bottom-Right** | Southeast | Suitability |
| **Center** | **Middle** | **TO EMBODY** <note>original: 體 (ti). In this context, it means to internalize or make these virtues part of one's own "body" or essence.</note> |
| **Bottom** | South | Forgetting |
| **Bottom-Left** | Southwest | Worry |
| **Left** | West | Wine |
| **Top-Left** | Northwest | Action |

<column-break/>

->**The Second Diagram: The Trust of Spirit**<-

| **Action** | **Direction** | **Instruction** |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Top** | North | Joy |
| **Top-Right** | Northeast | Sorrow |
| **Right** | East | Ease |
| **Bottom-Right** | Southeast | Attainment |
| **Center** | **Middle** | **TO ENTRUST** <note>original: 任 (ren). This suggests a philosophy of "allowing" or "yielding to" the natural flow of these states of being, a common theme in Wei-Jin "Pure Conversation" philosophy.</note> |
| **Bottom** | South | Virtue |
| **Bottom-Left** | Southwest | Acuity |
| **Left** | West | Wisdom |
| **Top-Left** | Northwest | Intoxication |

***

**Translator's Commentary:**
The layout of these inscriptions suggests they were intended to be read by joining the central character with any of the outer characters. For example, in the first diagram, one might read "Embody Wine" <note>original: 體酒 (ti jiu)</note> or "Embody Propriety" <note>original: 體禮 (ti li)</note>. The second diagram focuses on the internal state, suggesting one should "Entrust to Joy" <note>original: 任樂 (ren le)</note> or even "Entrust to Intoxication" <note>original: 任醉 (ren zui)</note>, balancing the rigid social rituals of the first plate with the spontaneous spirit of the second.

<summary>This page presents two circular philosophical inscriptions by the Jin Dynasty scholar Yin Zhongkan, intended to be engraved on a wine plate to guide the drinker's conduct and mindset.</summary>
<keywords>Yin Zhongkan, wine plate inscription, circular diagrams, Wei-Jin philosophy, ethics of drinking, calligraphy diagrams</keywords>

# Emperor Wu of Liang
<note>Emperor Wu (r. 502–549 AD) was the founding ruler of the Liang Dynasty. A dedicated patron of Buddhism and the arts, he was a prolific writer and poet who sought to infuse daily life with philosophical meaning.</note>

# Ancient Inkstone Inscription
<note>An inkstone inscription, or *yan ming*, is a short poetic text carved into the side or back of an inkstone. Because the scholar would see it every time they ground ink, it usually focused on themes of self-cultivation, clarity of mind, and the weight of words.</note>

->Diagram<-

| **Position** | **Translation** | **Original** |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Top** | Model | 模 |
| **Top-Right** | Virtue | 德 |
| **Right** | Write | 寫 |
| **Bottom-Right** | Heart | 心 |
| **Bottom** | Round | 圓 |
| **Bottom-Left** | Ink | 墨 |
| **Left** | Borrow | 假 |
| **Top-Left** | Formerly | 昔 |

<note>Read as a poem: "Formerly borrowing the roundness of the ink; the heart writes the model of virtue." Original: 昔假墨圓，心寫德模。</note>

---

->Disc<-
<note>The character "Bi" (壁) here refers to the "Yuanbi," a circular jade disc with a hole in the center, which serves as the visual inspiration for the diagram's layout.</note>

# Qiu Chi
<note>Qiu Chi (464–508 AD) was a famous official and man of letters during the Liang Dynasty, known for his elegant and emotionally resonant prose.</note>

# Inkstone Inscription

| **Position** | **Translation** | **Original** |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Top** | Firm | 剛 |
| **Top-Right** | Round | 圓 |
| **Right** | Water | 水 |
| **Bottom-Right** | Level | 平 |
| **Bottom** | Trace | 跡 |
| **Bottom-Left** | Truth | 真 |
| **Left** | Principle | 理 |
| **Top-Left** | Pattern | 則 |

<note>Read as a poem: "Firm and round like level water; these traces [of ink] are the patterns of truth." Original: 剛圓水平，跡真理則。</note>

<column-break/>

<note>The layout of these diagrams is more than decorative; they are "circular inscriptions" (*xuanwan*). Unlike linear text, the circular arrangement suggests the physical motion of the hand grinding ink in a circular path on the stone, merging the physical act of writing with the philosophical content of the poem.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates two inkstone inscriptions by prominent figures of the Liang Dynasty, presented in circular "jade-disc" diagrams that mirror the physical act of grinding ink. These poems emphasize the inkstone as a tool for moral self-cultivation and the expression of inner truth.</summary>

# Emperor Jianwen
<note>Emperor Jianwen (r. 549–551 AD), born Xiao Gang, was a ruler of the Liang Dynasty. He is best known in literary history as the primary architect of "Palace Style" poetry, a genre characterized by its delicate, sensory descriptions of objects and court life.</note>

# Gauze Fan Inscription
<note>A gauze fan (*shashan*) was a lightweight, translucent fan used by the nobility. Inscriptions on fans often compared the breeze they created or their circular shape to the moon and the elements of nature.</note>

->Diagram<-

| **Position** | **Translation** | **Original** |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Top** | Wind | 風 |
| **Top-Right** | Swaying | 颻 |
| **Right** | Radiant | 曜 |
| **Bottom-Right** | Light | 光 |
| **Bottom** | Royal | 王 |
| **Bottom-Left** | Moon | 月 |
| **Left** | Shining | 照 |
| **Top-Left** | Frost | 霜 |

<note>Read as a poem: "The wind sways with radiant light; the royal moon shines like frost." Original: 風颻曜光，王月照霜。 The "frost" refers to the cool temperature and the white, shimmering appearance of the gauze under light.</note>

---

# Tang Yin
<note>Tang Yin (1470–1524), popularly known by his courtesy name Tang Bohu, was a renowned painter, calligrapher, and poet of the Ming Dynasty. He is celebrated as one of the "Four Masters of Ming" and is a legendary figure in Chinese folklore.</note>

# Ink Inscription
<note>While previous inscriptions focused on the stone (*yan*), this text by Tang Yin focuses on the ink itself (*mo*). In Chinese art, ink is not merely a pigment but a medium that captures the spirit and "profound principles" of the universe.</note>

->Diagram<-

| **Position** | **Translation** | **Original** |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Top** | First | 先 |
| **Top-Right** | Elegant | 斐 |
| **Right** | Manifest | 徵 |
| **Bottom-Right** | Wondrous | 玅 |
| **Bottom** | Profound | 玄 |
| **Bottom-Left** | Principle | 理 |
| **Left** | Reside | 存 |
| **Top-Left** | Painting | 畫 |

<note>Read as a poem: "First elegance manifests the wondrous; profound principles reside in the painting." Original: 先斐徵玅，玄理存畫。 The character "玅" is an ancient variant of "妙" (wondrous/mysterious). The poem suggests that the beauty of art begins with elegant materials, but its true value lies in the philosophical truths preserved within the brushstrokes.</note>

# Emperor Taizong
<note>Emperor Taizong (r. 626–649 AD), born Li Shimin, was one of China’s greatest emperors. He is historically celebrated for his religious pluralism, famously welcoming the monk Xuanzang back from India and supporting the synthesis of Confucian, Buddhist, and Daoist thought.</note>

->The Way that is not ordinary is subtle and rare<-
<note>original: "非非常道希微" — This phrase combines the opening of the *Tao Te Ching* ("The Way that can be told is not the eternal Way") with terms for the subtle (*xi*) and the infinitesimal (*wei*), describing the elusive nature of ultimate truth.</note>

<image-desc>A semi-circular fan diagram divided into nine radiating segments within a rectangular frame. Each radial line separating the segments is capped at both its inner and outer ends with a decorative star-shaped floret. Chinese text is written horizontally in each segment, radiating outwards from the left. Outside the arc on the right are two large headings: "Emperor Taizong" at the top and "Palindrome" at the bottom. On the left side of the frame, there are two vertical lines of smaller text at the top and bottom.</image-desc>

| Segment | Translation | Original |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **1** | Harmoniously merging the tenets of Daoism, Buddhism, and Confucianism | 然融道佛儒旨 |
| **2** | A vessel to stir and cultivate great blessings | 航振介福培 |
| **3** | Greatness resides in opening the path of life | 大在開生活 |
| **4** | The Dragon’s instruction and wisdom transform and remove obstacles | 龍訓智摧移 |
| **5** | Teachings shine clearly with a new, eternal radiance | 教昭新永曜 |
| **6** | Guiding the lost through the honor of comparison | 指迷崇比擬 |
| **7** | Out of distress and hesitation, the old and new are born | 困徨生舊新 |
| **8** | Karma accompanies us, the light ahead is profound | 業伴前光深 |
| **9** | Virtuous vows transform all troubles and worries | 善願煩憂變 |

<note>The "Dragon's instruction" refers specifically to the Emperor’s own words or decrees.</note>

->Returning from the sea of suffering by following the inner spark<-
<note>original: "歸苦海自依機" — The <term>Sea of Suffering (Kuhai)</term> is a Buddhist metaphor for the cycle of birth and death (Samsara). "依機" (relying on the mechanism) suggests acting in accordance with spiritual opportunity or one's inner nature.</note>

# Palindrome
<note>A <term>Huiwen</term> (Palindrome) in Chinese literature is a sophisticated form of wordplay where a text can be read in various directions—forward, backward, or in a circle—while maintaining poetic meter and philosophical meaning. In this fan-shaped diagram, the radiating lines suggest a cyclical reading of the emperor's spiritual synthesis.</note>

<summary>This page presents a fan-shaped "palindrome" poem attributed to Emperor Taizong of the Tang Dynasty, expressing the harmony between Confucianism, Buddhism, and Daoism. The diagram illustrates the "Three Teachings" as a unified path to spiritual enlightenment and the relief of worldly suffering.</summary>

<keywords>Emperor Taizong, Three Teachings, Palindrome, Sancai Tuhui, Buddhism, Daoism, Confucianism, spiritual enlightenment</keywords>

# Imperial Composition
<note>original: "御製" (Yuzhi) — A term reserved for works personally composed by the Emperor. In the context of the Ming-era *Sancai Tuhui*, these attributions celebrate the literary legacy of past rulers.</note>

<image-desc>A semi-circular diagram is positioned on the right side of the page. It consists of nine radial lines (rays) extending from a central semi-circle towards an outer border. Each ray contains five Chinese characters, with the text oriented along the lines towards the center. The first character of the topmost ray is enclosed in a decorative, cloud-like circular frame. The overall layout suggests a poetic or mnemonic structure.</image-desc>

->Compassionate grace, words flowing like rain<-
<note>original: "慈恩言而淋" — Refers to the "rain of the law" or the Emperor's mercy reaching the people.</note>
->Proclaiming and spreading virtuous conduct<-
->Authentic practice transcends fame and profit<-
<note>original: "真實行名利" — Suggests that true spiritual or moral work is not done for worldly gain.</note>
->Principles arise, admired by both the internal and external world<-
->Through compassion and filial piety, may the people never falter<-
->Without morality and justice, one recognizes only the false<-
->Titles and designations arrive in diverse forms<-
->To gaze with greed is to wander in the external world<-
->The heart is stirred, delighting in composing and chanting<-

# A Thousand Stanzas
<note>original: "千首" — This likely refers to the "Collection of a Thousand Poems," a traditional way of titling large imperial anthologies.</note>

| Technical Term | English Translation | Explanation |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Imperial Composition** | 御製 (Yuzhi) | Works written by the Emperor's own hand or command. |
| **Thousand Stanzas** | 千首 (Qian Shou) | A common title for a large anthology of poetry. |
| **Compassionate Grace** | 慈恩 (Ci'en) | A Buddhist-inflected term for mercy; also the name of a famous temple in Xi'an. |
| **Virtuous Conduct** | 德行 (Dexing) | The outward manifestation of inner moral character. |
| **Absolute Truth** | 真實 (Zhenshi) | In this context, it refers to ultimate reality or "the way things truly are." |
| **Internal and External** | 中外 (Zhongwai) | Historically referring to China and foreign lands, or the inner self and the outer world. |
| **Compassion and Filial Piety** | 慈孝 (Cixiao) | The two pillars of family and social harmony in Confucian thought. |
| **Morality and Justice** | 道義 (Daoyi) | The ethical principles that guide a righteous person. |
| **Titles and Designations** | 稱謂 (Chengwei) | The names and ranks that define social and political standing. |

<summary>This page features an imperial poem by Emperor Taizong of Tang, presented in a radial "fan" layout that emphasizes the interconnectedness of virtue, truth, and governance. The text reflects a synthesis of Buddhist mercy and Confucian morality typical of the Tang court's intellectual style.</summary>
<keywords>Emperor Taizong, Imperial Poetry, Palindromic Verse, Virtuous Conduct, Tang Dynasty literature, Sancai Tuhui, Calligraphy Diagrams</keywords>

# Mirror Diagram of Entwined Grass
<note>original: "盤草鑑圖" (Pancao Jian Tu) — A title referring to a "revolving" poem, where the lines are "entwined" like grass in a mirror, implying a reflective and repeating structure.</note>

->Eight characters upon the flowers, eight characters between the branches, revolving in a cycle<-
<note>This refers to the visual layout where characters are placed on circular "flower" hubs and the "branches" that connect them.</note>
->Read in phrases of four characters; the rhyme remains constant throughout<-

***

### The Celestial Arrangement
<note>The diagram is framed by the Eight Trigrams of the *I Ching* (Book of Changes), which anchor the poem in the natural and spiritual order of the universe.</note>

| Trigram | Element/Meaning | Position on Page |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Wind** (巽 *Sun*) | Flexibility and penetration | Top |
| **Fire** (離 *Li*) | Light and intelligence | Center |
| **Water** (坎 *Kan*) | Depth and flow | Bottom center |
| **Lake** (兌 *Dui*) | Joy and openness | Top Left |
| **Earth** (坤 *Kun*) | Receptivity and nourishment | Middle Left |
| **Thunder** (震 *Zhen*) | Energy and movement | Bottom Left |

***

### The Five Centers
<note>Each "flower" in the diagram represents a core concept. The central character is surrounded by eight "petals" (characters) that can be read in a circle to form poetic lines.</note>

**I. The Center of Fragrance** (芬)
*Petals:* Rare, Ancient, Cloud, Strange, Path, Deep, Scent, Azure
<note>original: "奇、古、雲、怪、徑、深、芳、蒼"</note>

**II. The Center of Illumination** (照)
*Petals:* Brushing, Snow, Clearing, Dawn, Shadow, Front, Sunny, River
<note>original: "拂、雪、霽、曉、影、前、晴、川"</note>

**III. The Center of Purity** (潔)
*Petals:* Green, Flow, Current, Drawing, Pond, Deep, River, Jade-green
<note>original: "綠、流、派、引、池、深、江、碧"</note>

**IV. The Center of Emptiness** (空)
*Petals:* This, Simple, Ancient, Revere, You, Level, Still, Union
<note>original: "斯、簡、古、敬、爾、平、靜、合"</note>

**V. The Center of Clarity** (清)
*Petals:* Bright, Sacred, Resonant, Season, Change, Generation, Splendor, Bell
<note>original: "朗、聖、振、時、變、代、華、鍾"</note>

***

### The Connecting Branches
<note>These characters bridge the gaps between the circular "flowers," allowing the reader to navigate from one conceptual hub to the next.</note>

*   **From Fragrance to Illumination:** The crane's cry, brocade gauze, pasted in love, draped.
    <note>original: "鶴、唳、錦、紗、糊、戀、披"</note>
*   **From Fragrance to Purity:** Butterfly shadows flutter through thickets, drunk on enchanted stamens.
    <note>original: "蝶、影、翻、叢、蕊、醉、迷"</note>
*   **From Illumination to Emptiness:** Beaded curtains hang inverted, slanted shadows piercing through.
    <note>original: "珠、簾、倒、挂、斜、影、透"</note>
*   **From Purity to Clarity:** The mirror stand reaches for the subtle, heaven prolonging one's thoughts.
    <note>original: "鏡、臺、參、妙、天、延、想"</note>
*   **From Emptiness to Clarity:** Fish leap and waves overturn, the sound echoing to the very bottom.
    <note>original: "魚、躍、翻、波、聲、徹、底"</note>

<summary>This page presents a "Mirror Diagram of Entwined Grass," a sophisticated visual poem (palindrome) where characters arranged in "flowers" and "branches" can be read in multiple directions. The structure integrates poetic art with the metaphysical symbols of the I Ching trigrams.</summary>
<keywords>palindromic poem, 迴文詩, I Ching, Trigrams, Mirror Diagram, Chinese poetics, visual literature</keywords>

# The Mirror Diagram of Entwined Grass: The Eight Branches
<note>original: "盤草鑑圖" (Pancao Jian Tu) — See previous page for title details. This section focuses on the "winding and twisting" (盤屈糾結) nature of the circular path.</note>

### Instructions for the Winding Path

The path that winds and twists into eight branches should be read as follows:

Reading **counter-clockwise** <note>original: "左旋" (left-turning)</note>, start from the character **Composition** <note>original: "篇" (Pian)</note> and end at the character **Composition**. This reading follows the **"Xian" rhyme group**.
<term>Xian Rhyme (先仙字韻): A specific group of sounds in classical Chinese phonology used to ensure the poem sounds harmonious when read aloud.</term>

Reading **clockwise** <note>original: "右旋" (right-turning)</note>, start from the character **Verse** <note>original: "詞" (Ci)</note> and end at the character **Verse**. This reading follows the **"Zhi" rhyme group**.
<term>Zhi Rhyme (支脂字韻): Another distinct rhyme category; changing the reading direction shifts the poem into a completely different musical key.</term>

***

### The Cosmic Framework
<note>The poem is physically surrounded by the Eight Trigrams, anchoring the human virtues described in the text within the larger movements of the universe.</note>

| Trigram | Element/Attribute | Position in Diagram |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Heaven** (☰ *Qian*) | Strength and Creativity | Top Right |
| **Fire** (☲ *Li*) | Intelligence and Warmth | Middle Left |
| **Earth** (☷ *Kun*) | Receptivity and Foundation | Center |
| **Lake** (☱ *Dui*) | Joy and Interaction | Middle Right |
| **Water** (☵ *Kan*) | Depth and Mystery | Bottom Right |

***

### The Four Great Realms
<note>The winding text flows through four circular "hubs," each representing a different aspect of time and brilliance.</note>

**I. The Realm of the Year** (歲)
**II. The Realm of the Day** (日)
**III. The Realm of Radiance** (耀)
**IV. The Realm of Light** (光)

***

### The Path of Virtue and Diligence
<note>As the reader follows the path through the loops, they encounter characters that form a narrative of moral self-cultivation. The following words are woven into the "grass-like" path:</note>

-> Gate · Village · Cautious · Guard · Diligent · Labor · Early · Night · Think · Cultivate · Careful · Reach · Solid · Firm · Thick · Tranquil · Dwell · Peace · Quiet · Still · Silent · Ritual · Pass · Already · Celebrate · People · First · Allow · Lamp · Burn · Blaze · Sequence · Shine · Bright · Glow · Unite · After · Steps · Exalted · Splendor · Adorn · Manifest · Radiance · Brilliance <-

<note>The vocabulary emphasizes a lifestyle of constant vigilance ("Early and Night"), internal peace ("Tranquil," "Still"), and the eventual outward manifestation of one's character ("Radiance," "Splendor"). It suggests that by "Guarding the Gate" of one's mind with "Diligence," one eventually reaches a state of "Brilliance."</note>

<column-break/ >

<margin>
**Starting Points**
The markers **Composition** (篇) and **Verse** (詞) act as the "keys" to the map, telling the reader where to enter the winding labyrinth of text.
</margin>

<summary>This page illustrates a "revolving poem" diagram where a single string of text can be read in two directions to produce different poems with distinct rhyme schemes. The content links moral discipline and daily diligence to cosmic order and spiritual illumination.</summary>
<keywords>revolving poem, huiwen shi, Eight Trigrams, Xian rhyme, Zhi rhyme, self-cultivation, Chinese phonology, circular poetry</keywords>

# The Mirror Diagram of Entwined Grass: The Eight Branches
<note>original: "盤草鑑圖" (Pancao Jian Tu) — This section focuses on the "winding and twisting" (盤屈糾結) nature of the circular path.</note>

### Instructions for the Winding Path

The path that winds and twists into eight branches should be read as follows:

Reading **counter-clockwise** <note>original: "左旋" (left-turning)</note>, start from the character **Composition** <note>original: "篇" (Pian)</note> and end at the character **Composition**. This reading follows the **"Xian" rhyme group**.
<term>Xian Rhyme (先仙字韻): A specific group of sounds in classical Chinese phonology used to ensure the poem sounds harmonious when read aloud.</term>

Reading **clockwise** <note>original: "右旋" (right-turning)</note>, start from the character **Verse** <note>original: "詞" (Ci)</note> and end at the character **Verse**. This reading follows the **"Zhi" rhyme group**.
<term>Zhi Rhyme (支脂字韻): Another distinct rhyme category; changing the reading direction shifts the poem into a completely different musical key.</term>

# Bai Juyi’s Visit to the Purple Heaven Palace
<note>Bai Juyi (772–846), also known by his courtesy name Letian, was a renowned Tang Dynasty poet. The "Purple Heaven Palace" refers to a celestial Daoist realm or a specific temple of that name.</note>

<margin>
A "hidden-head" circular poem in the "character-splitting" style.
</margin>

<margin>
For each line, take the bottom half of the character above it to serve as the starting word. For example, if the character is *Ying* (滎), use the "Water" (水) component at its base. The final line uses the bottom of *Ying* as its rhyme.
</margin>

->The Jade Chain Ring<-

<image-desc>
A circular diagram of a poem arranged in an oval, titled "Jade Chain Ring" (玉連環). The text is intended to be read following a "hidden head" and "character-splitting" (藏頭拆字) rule, where the bottom component of a character from the previous line becomes the starting character for the next. The characters are arranged in a continuous loop.
</image-desc>

> **[Water]** washes away the dust, the flavor of the Way is rare;
> **[At]** fame and profit, the two are mutually forgotten.
> **[The heart]** cherishes the guests of the Six Caves and Cinnabar Clouds;
> **[The mouth]** chants to the Lords of the Three Pure Purple Palaces.
> **[Within]** the forest, lotus songs last until dawn;
> **[The sun]** yields to the bright moon, and the cassia wafts its fragrance.
> **[Daily]** the high lords return to meet one another;
> **[One]** finds that within the mountains, it is good.

<note>
This translation reconstructs the "hidden" poem described by the character-splitting rules. The bracketed words are derived from the components of the characters:
1. "Water" (水) from the bottom of *Ying* (滎).
2. "At/In" (于) from the bottom of *Han* (罕).
3. "Heart" (心) from the bottom of *Wang* (忘).
4. "Mouth" (口) from the bottom of *Ke* (客).
5. "Inch/Within" (寸) from the bottom of *Zun* (尊).
6. "Sun" (日) from the bottom of *Dan* (旦).
7. "Sun/Daily" (日) from the bottom of *Xiang* (香).
8. "One/Man" (儿) from the bottom of *Jian* (見).
</note>

***

<summary>This page illustrates a "Jade Chain Ring" poem, a complex literary puzzle attributed to the poet Bai Juyi, which requires the reader to "split" characters to find the start of each line. It also provides instructions for navigating circular diagrams using the Eight Trigrams and specific rhyme groups.</summary>
<keywords>Bai Juyi, Jade Chain Ring, character-splitting poem, Daoism, Purple Heaven Palace, Huiwen, Eight Trigrams, classical Chinese poetics</keywords>

# Song An’s Poetic Gift to Fan Zhongyan
<note>Fan Zhongyan (989–1052) was a preeminent scholar-official of the Song Dynasty, known for his administrative reforms and his famous line: "Be the first to worry about the world's troubles and the last to enjoy its pleasures."</note>

<image-desc>A circular diagram containing twenty-one characters arranged in a ring. This is a circular palindrome poem designed to be read in a continuous loop. The characters are: Prefecture (州), Willow (柳), Hanging (垂), Silk (絲), Brushing (惹), Lonely (孤), Plucking (摘), Faint (微), Grievance (辜), River (江), Open (開), Flower (花), Flying (飛), Abundant (繁), Shadow (影), Tangled (亂), Hemp (麻), Wild (野), Vast (闊), Several (幾), Standing (立).</image-desc>

<column-break/>

**The character "Flower" hides the head;**
**Use two calls and three summons.**
<note>This refers to the "Hidden Head" (藏頭 *Cangtou*) style. The reader must find the character "Flower" (花) and use it as the starting point or pivot, repeating certain phrases to complete the poetic meter.</note>

**Five or seven characters form the verse;**
**It flows through from left to right.**
<term>Left and Right Flow (左右通貫): This indicates that the poem is perfectly symmetrical in its linguistic structure, allowing it to be read in either direction around the circle while maintaining poetic meaning and rhythm.</term>

***

### Reading the Diagram
To unlock the poem, one might begin at the character **Flower** (花) and follow the circle. A possible reading in seven-character meter <note>original: "七言" (qiyan)</note> would be:

*Flowers open, the river [is] a faint grievance,*
*Plucking the lonely [reeds], brushing the hanging silk willows.*
*The isle stands, several vast wilds [stretch out],*
*Hemp shadows tangle, flying in abundance.*

<note>This translation represents one possible path. The beauty of these diagrams lies in how the "tangled" (亂) and "abundant" (繁) imagery mirrors the physical difficulty of deciphering the circular text.</note>

<vocab>
**Prefecture/Isle** (州), **Willow** (柳), **Hanging** (垂), **Silk** (絲), **Brushing** (惹), **Lonely** (孤), **Plucking** (摘), **Faint** (微), **Grievance** (辜), **River** (江), **Open** (開), **Flower** (花), **Flying** (飛), **Abundant** (繁), **Shadow** (影), **Tangled** (亂), **Hemp** (麻), **Wild** (野), **Vast** (闊), **Several** (幾), **Standing** (立)
</vocab>

<summary>This page illustrates a circular "palindrome poem" sent to the statesman Fan Zhongyan, showcasing a "Hidden Head" structure where the poem's meaning is unlocked by starting at the character for "Flower." It demonstrates the sophisticated wordplay and visual literacy expected among the Song Dynasty elite.</summary>

<keywords>circular poem, Fan Zhongyan, Song An, Sancai Tuhui, palindrome, Hidden Head poetry, Song Dynasty literature</keywords>

# Borrowed Character Palindromic Style

## A Traveler’s Longing

<image-desc>A circular diagram containing fourteen traditional Chinese characters. The characters are arranged in a ring to be read in a continuous, interlocking loop. Starting from the top and moving clockwise, the characters are: Longing (憶), Separated (隔), Date (期), Departure (別), Parting (離), Hour (時), Hear (聞), Water-clock (漏), Turning (轉), Quiet (靜), Thinking (思), Her (伊), Long (久), and Blocked (阻).</image-desc>

->Taixu, Qin Guan<-
<note>Qin Guan (1049–1100), courtesy name Taixu, was one of the most celebrated lyric poets of the Northern Song Dynasty. He was a member of the "Four Scholars of Su Shi" and was renowned for his evocative, often melancholic, poetry regarding love and exile.</note>

<column-break/>

<term>Borrowed Character Palindrome (借字迴文體): A poetic form where the last character of a line is "borrowed" to serve as the first character of the succeeding line. In this circular arrangement, the chain of characters creates a never-ending cycle of verse.</term>

### Deciphering the Poem

To read this "revolving" verse, one follows the circle clockwise, using each third character to begin the next phrase:

**Longing for the distant date,**
**The date of our departure and parting.**
**At the hour of parting, I hear—**
**Hearing the water-clock turn,**
**Turning to quiet thoughts.**
**Thinking of her for a long time,**
**Long blocked by longing.**
<note>This translation follows the 3-character "interlocking" structure typical of this diagram. The water-clock (漏) refers to the clepsydra used in ancient China to measure time, its dripping often symbolizing the slow, painful passage of time for someone in love or in exile.</note>

***

**Notes on the Composition:**
The poem captures the restless mind of a traveler ("Guest's Longing"). The circularity of the text reflects the circularity of his thoughts—returning constantly to the moment of separation and the image of the beloved. By using the <gloss>Borrowed Character</gloss> (借字 *jiezi*) method, Qin Guan ensures that the poem has no definitive beginning or end, mirroring the "unbroken thread" of the traveler's sorrow.

<summary>This page illustrates the "Borrowed Character" palindromic style through a 14-character circular poem by Qin Guan, where lines interlock to depict the repetitive, circular nature of a traveler's grief.</summary>
<keywords>Qin Guan, Borrowed Character Style, Palindrome, Song Dynasty Poetry, Water-clock, Sancai Tuhui, Interlocking Verse</keywords>

While the previous page focused on the structural ingenuity of Song Dynasty poets like Qin Guan, this page provides a foundational map of the literary traditions that informed such mastery, tracing the lineage of Chinese verse from its ancient origins to the peak of the Tang Dynasty.</meta>

# Diagram of the Origins and Lineage of Poetry Studies

<image-desc>
A structural lineage chart titled "Diagram of the Origins and Lineage of Poetry Studies." The chart is organized into vertical columns separated by lines, tracing the descent of various poetic schools and major poets from the foundational "Classic of Poetry" (Shijing). The layout features a central axis with branching paths representing the evolution of different stylistic traditions.
</image-desc>

| | | | | | | | |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Tao Qian** <note>Tao Qian (365–427), also known as Tao Yuanming, is the forefather of "fields and gardens" poetry, celebrated for his themes of reclusion and nature.</note> | **The Huangchu Era** <note>The reign period (220–226 CE) of Cao Pi of the Wei Dynasty, a foundational era for refined pentasyllabic poetry.</note> | **Li Ling** <note>A Han Dynasty general; poems attributed to him are among the earliest examples of the five-character line style.</note> | **Ancient Style Poetry** <note>original: *Gushi* (古詩) — refers specifically to the formative "Nineteen Old Poems" of the Han Dynasty.</note> | **The Three Hundred Poems** <note>original: *Sanbaipian* (三百篇) — A traditional name for the *Shijing* (Classic of Poetry), the oldest collection of Chinese verse and the source of all later tradition.</note> | **Music Bureau Style** <note>original: *Yuefu* (樂府) — Folk-ballad style poetry originally collected by the Han Dynasty Music Bureau.</note> | **Su Wu** <note>A Han official (d. 60 BCE) famed for his loyalty; his "parting poems" are foundational to the classical canon.</note> | **The Three Xies** <note>Refers to Xie Lingyun, Xie Huilian, and Xie Tiao, masters of landscape poetry during the 5th and 6th centuries.</note> |
| | **Bao Zhao** <note>A Southern Dynasties poet known for his energetic, innovative style and "hardships of travel" themes.</note> | **Zuo Si** <note>A Western Jin poet famous for his "Rhapsodies on the Three Capitals" and his powerful, gritty "History" poems.</note> | **Pan Yue** <note>A Western Jin poet celebrated for his exquisite craftsmanship and his influential laments for his late wife.</note> | **Songs of Chu** <note>original: *Chuci* (楚詞) — The second great ancient anthology, originating from the southern state of Chu, characterized by its romanticism and shamanic imagery.</note> | **Ruan Ji** <note>One of the Seven Sages of the Bamboo Grove, famous for his profound and often cryptic "Poems from My Heart."</note> | **The Two Lus** <note>Brothers Lu Ji and Lu Yun, prominent literary figures and theorists of the Western Jin Dynasty.</note> | **The Jian’an Style** <note>Named for the era (196–220 CE), this style is marked by vigor, emotional intensity, and social concern.</note> |
| | | | | **Liu Kun** <note>A Western Jin general and poet whose works express the tragic heroism of a falling empire.</note> | | | **Guo Pu** <note>A scholar and poet (276–324) known for his "Immortal-Roaming" poems blending Daoism and nature.</note> |
| | | | | **Du Fu** <note>The "Poet-Sage" (712–770) of the Tang Dynasty, seen here as the ultimate inheritor and synthesizer of the entire poetic tradition.</note> | | | |

<column-break/>

<term>Poetry Studies (詩學 - Shixue): The historical and theoretical study of verse, focusing on the lineage of influence from ancient classics to later masters.</term>

<note>This chart serves as a pedagogical tool to help students of literature understand how diverse styles—such as the rustic simplicity of Tao Qian or the vigorous "Jian'an" spirit—all spring from the twin fountains of the *Classic of Poetry* and the *Songs of Chu*. By placing Du Fu at the base of the central column, the diagram asserts that his work represents the culmination of all these historical developments.</note>

<summary>This page presents a genealogical chart tracing the development of Chinese poetry from its foundational classics to the High Tang master Du Fu. It maps the relationships between historical eras, specific genres like Yuefu, and canonical poets, illustrating the "origins and lineage" of the Chinese literary tradition.</summary>
<keywords>literary lineage, Shijing, Chuci, Du Fu, Tao Qian, Jian'an poetry, Yuefu, history of Chinese literature</keywords>

<meta>This page provides the textual narrative that accompanies the "Diagram of the Origins and Lineage of Poetry Studies" introduced on the previous page. It categorizes the history of Chinese verse into three distinct tiers: the ancient "ancestors" who established the forms, the "lineage" of refined masters who perfected them, and the "great culmination" found in the work of Du Fu.</meta>

### Collected Illustrations of the Three Realms
## Literature and History: Volume 3

->Page 34<-

Regarding the various forms of poetry: The **Three Hundred Poems** <note>original: *Sanbaipian* (三百篇); a traditional name for the *Classic of Poetry* (*Shijing*), the oldest collection of Chinese verse dating from the 11th to 7th centuries BCE</note> flowed into the **Lyrics of Chu** <note>original: *Chuci* (楚詞); the expressive, shamanistic poetry of the south associated with Qu Yuan</note>, then into the **Music Bureau** <note>original: *Yuefu* (樂府); folk-ballads originally collected by the Han Dynasty court</note> style, the **Nineteen Old Poems** <note>the definitive collection of Han Dynasty pentasyllabic verse</note>, the **five-character verse of Su Wu and Li Ling**, and the styles of the **Jian'an** and **Huangchu** eras <note>reign periods (196–226 CE) during the transition from the Han to the Wei Dynasty, known for robust and emotional poetry</note>. These constitute the **ancestors of poetry**.

The various poems of **Liu Kun**, **Ruan Ji**, **Pan Yue**, **Lu Ji**, **Zuo Si**, **Guo Pu**, **Bao Zhao**, and **Xie Lingyun** <note>these are the major masters of the 3rd through 5th centuries, representing the peak of medieval "high" style</note> as preserved in the ***Selections of Refined Literature*** <note>original: *Wen Xuan* (文選); the foundational anthology of early Chinese literature compiled by Prince Xiao Tong</note>, along with the ***Collected Works of Yuanming*** <note>The poet Tao Qian (365–427 CE), celebrated for his simplicity and themes of rural reclusion</note>, represent the **orthodox lineage of poetry**.

The ***Collected Works of Old Du*** <note>Referring to Du Fu (712–770 CE), the Tang Dynasty poet often called the "Poet-Historian"</note> represents the **great culmination** <note>original: *Dacheng* (大成); a term used to describe a master who has synthesized all previous traditions into a single, perfect whole</note> of the poetic tradition.

<summary>This page defines the historical progression of Chinese poetry, identifying the "Classic of Poetry" as its root and the Tang Dynasty poet Du Fu as the ultimate synthesis of the entire tradition.</summary>
<keywords>Classic of Poetry, Yuefu, Wen Xuan, Tao Yuanming, Du Fu, Poetic Lineage, Jian'an Style</keywords>

--- Page 70 ---
<meta>Following the discussion of the historical "ancestors" of poetry—the *Classic of Poetry* and the *Selections of Refined Literature*—this page transitions into the *Ci* (Lyric) genre. It provides a formal template and examples for a specific "tune title," illustrating how the poetic tradition evolved into the musical and metrical structures of later dynasties.</meta>

詞

<note>original: *Ci*; a genre of classical Chinese poetry originally written to be sung to specific musical tunes. Unlike earlier forms, *Ci* features lines of varying lengths (long and short), earning it the nickname "long and short lines."</note>

The spring rain is cold and dreary. A pale mist deeply locks the courtyard of weeping willows. The warm breeze gently fans. The peach blossom petals have all fallen away.

Anonymous

<column-break/>

Twenty-one characters

<image-desc>A prosody diagram for the Lyric tune "Touching Red Lips" (*Dian Jiang Chun*). It consists of vertical columns of circles representing the required tones for each character: solid black circles for <term>oblique tones (Ze)</term>, hollow circles for <term>level tones (Ping)</term>, and half-filled circles for tones that can be either. Small text labels indicate the phrase structure: "First phrase: four characters," "Second phrase: seven characters, beginning with an oblique rhyme," "Third phrase: five characters, rhyming in oblique," and "Fourth phrase: five characters, rhyming in oblique." For the second stanza, the annotations specify lengths of three, four, and five characters with associated rhyme instructions.</image-desc>

# Touching Red Lips

<note>original: *Dian Jiang Chun*; a famous tune title in *Ci* poetry. The title literally means "Dabbing Red on the Lips," evoking the image of a lady applying makeup.</note>

First stanza: four lines, three rhymes, twenty-one characters. Second stanza: five lines, four rhymes.

<column-break/>

Clouds easily drift away. Broken with grief, I know not where to turn. The deep courtyard locks in the twilight. Burst after burst of rain falls upon the plantains <note>The sound of rain hitting the large, waxy leaves of the plantain or banana tree is a frequent symbol of loneliness and sorrow in Chinese literature.</note>.

<summary>This page introduces the *Ci* (lyric) genre through the specific tune "Touching Red Lips," providing both poetic examples and a metrical diagram for its construction. The imagery focuses on "locked" courtyards, falling blossoms, and evening rain, which are hallmark themes of the melancholy "Inner Quarters" style of lyric poetry.</summary>
<keywords>Ci poetry, Lyric, Touching Red Lips, Dian Jiang Chun, prosody, metrical patterns, Chinese aesthetics, plantain rain</keywords>

I am unlucky that he does not come; I think over past events again and again. With no way to see him, my tear-streaks are like lines, cutting through my fading makeup.

# Good Spring Scenery
<note>original: *Chun Guang Hao*; a tune title for *Ci* poetry. Tune titles dictate the specific rhythm, rhyme scheme, and line lengths the poet must follow, regardless of the poem's actual subject matter.</note>

->First stanza: five lines, four rhymes, nineteen characters. Second stanza: four lines, two rhymes.<-
->Twenty-two characters.<-

<margin>Level tone</margin>
<margin>Rhyme</margin>

<image-desc>A metrical chart for the "Good Spring Scenery" (*Chun Guang Hao*) poetic form. The chart uses vertical columns of hollow (○) and solid (●) circles to indicate the required tones (level and oblique) for each character in the poem. Small-character annotations provide structural details: "First line, 3 characters," "Second line, 3 characters, starting with a level rhyme," "Third line, 3 characters, level rhyme," "Fourth line, 7 characters, level rhyme," "Fifth line, 3 characters." The second stanza structure follows: "Starting line, 6 characters," "Second line, 6 characters, level rhyme," "Third line, 7 characters," and "Fourth line, 3 characters."</image-desc>

# Silk-Washing Stream
<note>original: *Huan Xi Sha*; one of the most popular tune titles for lyric poetry. The name originates from the legend of Xi Shi, a legendary beauty of ancient China who washed silk by a stream.</note>

->First stanza: three lines, three rhymes, twenty-one characters. Second stanza: three lines, two rhymes, twenty-one characters.<-

Small interlinear glosses specify the structure of each line.</image-desc>

->Lyric Poetry<-

->Qin Shaoyou<-
<note>Qin Shaoyou (1049–1100), also known as Qin Guan, was a celebrated poet of the Northern Song Dynasty, famous for his delicate and often sorrowful "Ci" poetry.</note>

Brocade curtains, layer upon layer, are rolled up like evening clouds.
The folding screen, curve upon curve, is closed with red ivory clasps.
<note>original: "hong ya" (red ivory); this likely refers to decorative clasps on a screen or perhaps castanets used to keep time for music.</note>
I resent my love—for what reason must he suffer leaving home?

On the pillow, my dreaming soul cannot fly away to find him.
Waking up, the red sun is slanting toward the west again.
The garden full of fragrant grass sets off the fallen blossoms.

<term>Silk-Washing Stream (Huan Xi Sha)</term>: A poetic form consisting of two stanzas of three lines each, all seven characters long. <term>Qin Shaoyou</term>: The courtesy name of Qin Guan, a master of the "elegant" style of lyric poetry. <term>Lyric Poetry (Ci)</term>: A genre of Chinese poetry written to fit set musical melodies. <term>Rhyme (Yun)</term>: The phonetic correspondence between the ends of lines. <term>Level Rhyme (Ping Xie)</term>: A rhyme using the "level" or "even" tone, which sounds sustained and musical. <term>Brocade Curtains (Jin Zhang)</term>: Luxurious silk hangings. <term>Evening Clouds (Mu Xia)</term>: The colorful clouds seen at sunset. <term>Folding Screen (Ping Feng)</term>: A piece of furniture used for privacy or decoration in traditional homes. <term>Red Ivory (Hong Ya)</term>: High-status decorative material. <term>Dream Soul (Meng Hun)</term>: The part of the spirit believed to travel and see loved ones during sleep. <term>Fallen Blossoms (Can Hua)</term>: Flowers that have begun to wither and drop, a common symbol of passing time or lost beauty.

<summary>This page concludes a poem by He Ning and provides the metrical structure and a famous example for the "Silk-Washing Stream" (Huan Xi Sha) poetic form. The featured poem by Qin Guan expresses the melancholy of a woman longing for her husband who has traveled away for work or duty.</summary>

# Deepening Affection
<note>original: *Lian Qing Shen*; a tune title (cipai) used for lyric poetry. The name suggests a theme of profound or lingering romantic attachment.</note>

## Front stanza: four lines, four rhymes, twenty-one characters. Back stanza: four lines, three rhymes, twenty-one characters.

<image-desc>
A metrical tonal diagram for the "Deepening Affection" tune. The chart uses vertical columns of circles to denote the required rhythm. Level tones (ping) are usually represented by empty circles, while oblique tones (ze) are filled. The annotations provide the structural requirements for each line:
- Column 1: <gloss>First line: seven characters, does not start with a rhyme</gloss>
- Column 2: <gloss>Second line: four characters, rhyming in a level tone</gloss>
- Column 3: <gloss>Third line: three characters, rhyming in a level tone</gloss>
- Column 4: <gloss>Fourth line: seven characters, level-tone rhyme</gloss>
- Column 5: <gloss>Opening line of second stanza: seven characters</gloss>
- Column 6: <gloss>Second line: five characters</gloss>
- Column 7: <gloss>Third line: three characters</gloss>
- Column 8: <gloss>Final line: six characters</gloss> <note>The accompanying poem uses a three-character ending here; metrical charts often reflect one of several regional or historical variations.</note>
</image-desc>

->Lyric Poetry: Palace Lyrics<-
<term>Palace Lyrics: *Gong Ci*; a genre of poetry describing the luxurious, often lonely or highly ritualized life of women and attendants within the imperial palace.</term>

->Mao Wenxi<-
<note>Mao Wenxi (active c. 9th–10th century) was a government official and poet of the Former Shu kingdom during the Five Dynasties and Ten Kingdoms period.</note>

In the jade palace, spring is lush and the flowers are in brilliant bloom,
Clustered together with divine companions.
Their fine silk robes are narrow and elegant,
As gold threads shimmer while they play clear, melodic notes.

The wine is finished and the songs have ceased; both lovers feel a heavy drowse.
With a single smile, she moves the Emperor's heart.
She does not yet wish to be merely a pair of mandarin ducks,
For her love runs deep.
<note>Mandarin ducks are a traditional Chinese symbol for a devoted married couple. Here, the poet suggests her feelings are even more profound than this standard symbol of fidelity.</note>

**Lyric**
<term>Lyric: *Ci*; a form of Classical Chinese poetry written to fit the rhythm and melody of specific musical tunes.</term>

Pass and river filled with sorrowful thoughts as far as the eye can reach. 
Gradually, the pale autumn <note>original: *su qiu*; the "white" or "plain" autumn, reflecting the traditional association of autumn with the color white and the element of metal.</note> moves toward evening. 
Wild geese fly past the southern clouds. 
The traveler turns their tear-filled eyes.

Regretting spreading the "Double Phoenix" quilt <note>original: *Shuang luan qin chou*; a quilt embroidered with a pair of mythical birds, symbolizing marital union and intimacy.</note> tonight. 
Once again, a solitary person on a long pillow. 
Looking afar, the return is not yet realized. 
The plum blossoms hear the flute of the frontier pass. <note>The "frontier tube" or flute often played "Plum Blossoms Falling," a tune that triggered homesickness in soldiers and travelers.</note>

<meta>This page transitions from the previous discussion of the "Grievance of the Clear Mode" tune into a famous example of the lyric form attributed to the Tang Dynasty poet Li Bai. It then provides the formal metrical structure for the "Bodhisattva Barbarian" tune, continuing the manuscript's survey of poetic patterns.</meta>

## Lyric
<term>Lyric: *Ci*; a genre of poetry defined by lines of varying lengths, written to follow the rhythm and melody of existing musical tunes.</term>
### Li Taibai
<note>Li Taibai (701–762), more commonly known as Li Bai, is revered as one of the "Immortal Poets" of the Tang Dynasty.</note>

○ Flat forests stretch vast, the mist woven like silk. A range of cold mountains, a heartbreaking shade of emerald. Twilight hues enter the high tower; someone atop the tower is filled with sorrow.
○ Standing in vain upon the jade steps. Birds returning to roost fly in great haste. Where is the journey home? Past the long pavilion, then the short <unclear>pavilion</unclear>.
<note>In ancient China, "long and short pavilions" were designated rest stops along main roads, often serving as the sites for emotional farewells between friends and lovers.</note>

<column-break/>

# The Bodhisattva Barbarian 
<note>original: *Pusa Man*. This tune name is said to have originated during the Tang Dynasty when a tribute mission from a southern kingdom arrived wearing elaborate headdresses that resembled Buddhist icons (Bodhisattvas).</note>
<gloss>Also known as "Overlapping Gold," "Midnight Song," or as being similar to the tune "The Drunken Gentleman."</gloss>

## Front stanza: four lines, four rhymes, twenty-four characters
## Back stanza: four lines, four rhymes, twenty characters

<image-desc>
A metrical diagram for the "Bodhisattva Barbarian" tune pattern, outlining the tonal structure and rhyme scheme for two stanzas. 
- The right side defines the front stanza: it contains four vertical lines of 7, 7, 5, and 5 circles respectively. Annotations specify: "First line: 7 characters, starting with an oblique rhyme," "Second line: 7 characters, rhyming in the oblique tone," "Third line: 5 characters, switching to a level rhyme," and "Fourth line: 5 characters, rhyming in the level tone."
- The left side defines the back stanza: it contains four vertical lines of 5 circles each. Annotations specify: "Starting line: 5 characters, switching to an oblique rhyme," "Second line: 5 characters, rhyming in the oblique tone," "Third line: 5 characters, switching to a level rhyme," and "Fourth line: 5 characters, rhyming in the level tone."
The circles represent character tones: solid black for oblique (*ze*), hollow for level (*ping*), and half-filled for variable tones.
</image-desc>

<term>The Bodhisattva Barbarian (*Pusa Man*): A classic tune title in the *Ci* genre.</term>
<term>Lyric (*Ci*): Poetry following specific musical meters.</term>
<term>Li Taibai: The style name of the poet Li Bai.</term>
<term>Front Stanza (*Qianduan*): The first half of a lyric poem.</term>
<term>Back Stanza (*Houduan*): The second half of a lyric poem.</term>
<term>Oblique Rhyme (*Ze Yun*): A rhyme utilizing the "oblique" tones (rising, departing, or entering), which sound more forceful or clipped.</term>
<term>Level Rhyme (*Ping Yun*): A rhyme utilizing the "level" tone, which sounds more sustained and melodic.</term>
<term>Rhyme Correspondence (*Ye Yun*): The requirement for a line to match the rhyme established previously.</term>
<term>Rhyme Change (*Huan Yun*): The shifting from one rhyme group to another within a single poem.</term>

<summary>This page features a famous lyric poem attributed to Li Bai and provides a technical metrical guide for the "Bodhisattva Barbarian" tune pattern. It illustrates both the creative application and the rigid structural requirements of the Ci poetry genre.</summary>
<keywords>Li Bai, Ci poetry, Bodhisattva Barbarian, Pusa Man, prosody, metrical diagrams, Tang Dynasty literature, Chinese poetics</keywords>

**The Monk Zhongshu**
<note>Zhongshu (active late 11th century) was a famous Buddhist monk and poet known for his descriptions of the scenic West Lake in Hangzhou.</note>

Outside the Yongjin Gate lies Little Yingzhou; the Cold Food Festival there is even more elegant. Red boats fill the lake with the sound of singing and flutes; beyond the flowers, there is a high <unclear>pavilion</unclear>...

#### Lyric: Ascending the Tower of Wushan County
-> Huang Shangu <-
<note>Huang Shangu is the pseudonym of Huang Tingjian (1045–1105), a master poet and calligrapher of the Song Dynasty known for his complex and intellectual style.</note>

Within the dreams of King Xiang, the grass is green and the mist is deep. Where is the terrace of Song Yu? Evening rain and morning clouds—how much sorrow they bring!
<note>These refer to the legend of the Goddess of Wushan, who visited King Xiang of Chu in his dreams, appearing as morning clouds and evening rain. Song Yu was the poet who recorded the encounter.</note>

○ Flying petals drift endlessly; they care not that the traveler’s heart is breaking. The spring waters are vast and hazy; wishing to cross to Nanling, the path is even more severed.

<note>original: "減字木蘭花" (Shortened Magnolia), "詞牌" (Tune-title), "仄韻" (Oblique rhyme), "平韻" (Level rhyme), "黃山谷" (Huang Tingjian), "巫山" (Wushan Mountain), "宋玉" (Song Yu), "襄王" (King Xiang)</note>

### Lyric: Ode to Snow
#### Kang Bo-ke
<note>Kang Bo-ke, also known as Kang Yuzhi (active 12th century), was a Southern Song official and poet known for his descriptive lyrics.</note>

Feng Yi <note>original: "馮夷" (Feng Yi), the God of the Yellow River or water deity</note> has shredded the fine white silk of the clear stream. The snow flies down from the uniform gray clouds, touching the ground without leaving a trace. Like willow catkins or plum blossoms, it is as if spring is everywhere.

In Shanyin <note>A reference to a famous anecdote about the scholar Wang Huizhi, who traveled by boat through a snowy night to visit a friend, only to turn back at the door because his "whim" had ended</note>, this night is as bright as a painting. The moon is full over the village ahead; do not roll up the door by the stream. For I fear there may be someone in a small boat arriving on a whim.

# The Diviner <note>The original Chinese heading is "二字" (Two Characters), a shorthand for the meter *Bu Suan Zi* (The Diviner). The note "後段同前" indicates the second stanza follows the same metrical structure as the first.</note>

- First line: ●●●●○○ <gloss>First line: five characters</gloss>
- Second line: ⦿◐◑◐ <gloss>Second line: three characters</gloss>
- Third line: ○○○○○●● <gloss>Third line: seven characters</gloss>
- Fourth line: ●●●●○ <gloss>Fourth line: five characters, rhyming in the oblique tone</gloss>
<note>In these diagrams, circles represent syllables. A solid circle (●) is an oblique tone, an open circle (○) is a level tone, and modified circles (⦿/◑) indicate syllables where the tone may vary.</note>

->Lyric<-

->Qin Chudu<-
<note>Qin Chudu is the courtesy name of Qin Zhan (late 11th–early 12th century), the son of the famous Song dynasty poet Qin Guan.</note>

Spring permeates the clarity of the water waves; the cold is sharp, and the plum branches are thin. Looking as far as the eye can see at the hundred-foot tower shrouded in mist, the person inside the tower does not—

○ The "four-harmony" <note>A famous blended incense made of four ingredients: agarwood, sandalwood, ambergris, and musk</note> incense curls from the golden duck censer; the delicate hands are busy at the game of "Double Sixes." <note>An ancient board game similar to backgammon, popular among the Song dynasty elite</note> I intended to ask the east wind to wash away this emotion, but the emotion is even thicker than wine.

---

### Good News is Near
<term>Good News is Near: *Hao Shi Jin*. A lyric meter (*ci*) typically used for expressing lightheartedness, longing, or scenic beauty.</term>

The front stanza consists of four lines and two rhymes, totaling twenty-two characters. The back stanza consists of four lines and two rhymes, totaling twenty-three characters.

# Embroidered Sash <note>Original: *Xiu Dai Zi*. This is a *ci* meter, a poetic form written to a specific existing melody.</note>
The first stanza consists of four lines, three rhymes, and twenty-one characters.
The second stanza consists of five lines, three— <note>The text is cut off but refers to the rhyming structure of the second half.</note>

## Lyric: Early Summer
->Jiang Ziyun<-
<note>Jiang Ziyun was a poet of the Southern Song dynasty known for his delicate imagery.</note>

Leaves grow dark where the young crows cry.
The wind stills, yet the faded red blossoms still fall.
Butterflies do not depart with the passing spring;
They enter the pool-side pavilion on the fragrant southern breeze. <note>Original: *Xunfeng*, a literary term for the warm wind from the south that arrives in early summer.</note>

Cease singing "The Golden Thread Robe." <note>A famous Tang dynasty song urging the listener to cherish their youth while they can.</note>
I urge the golden goblet.
The weariness from wine is as heavy as yesterday.
The curtains are rolled up; the day is long and the person is quiet,
Letting the willow catkins drift where they may.

<column-break/>
