->Lyric on the Plum Blossom<-

->Huang Shangu<-
<note>Huang Shangu is the pseudonym of Huang Tingjian (1045–1105), one of the "Four Masters of the Song Dynasty," renowned for his innovative poetry and distinctive calligraphy.</note>

In the small courtyard, a single branch of plum blossoms.
Bursting open through the chill of the dawn.
Arriving late to wander in the fragrant garden,
I return with my sleeves filled with their scent. ○ <note>The circle indicates a rhyme point in the lyric.</note>

Jade-like wine overflows the silver cup.
I drink until fully intoxicated before leaving,
Yet I still look forward to coming back again.
For what reason does the eastern neighbor
Startle me by blowing that sorrowful flute? <note>A reference to the traditional flute melody "Falling Plum Blossoms," which evokes a sense of melancholy and the passing of time.</note>
The petals fall, like piles of snow.

<note>This lyric follows the "Embroidered Sash" meter. It captures a quintessential literati moment: admiring the first plum blossoms of late winter, drinking wine, and feeling a pang of poetic sadness at the sound of a flute, which reminds the poet that the blossoms will soon wither.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates the metrical diagram and rhyme scheme for a variation of the "Embroidered Sash" lyric meter, featuring a plum blossom-themed poem by the famous Song dynasty poet Huang Tingjian.</summary>
<keywords>Huang Tingjian, Huang Shangu, Sancai Tuhui, ci poetry, lyric meter, plum blossoms, prosody, Song dynasty literature</keywords>

<term>Gate of the Gazing Immortal (Wang Xian Men)</term>: A *cipai* or rhythmic template for song lyrics. <term>Yan Tongshu</term>: Referring to the poet Yan Shu. <term>Lyric Prosody (Cipu)</term>: A manual used by poets to ensure their words fit the musical requirements of the tune. <term>Level Rhyme (Ping Yun)</term>: A rhyme using the "level" tone, which was sustained and musical. <term>Rhyme Match (Ye)</term>: The requirement for a line to harmonize with the established rhyme of the poem. <term>Lyric Law (Cilü)</term>: The formal rules governing the structure of these poems.

->The lyric meter is also known as "A String of Falling Pearls"<- <note>Original: *Yi luo suo*.</note>

->By Chen Houshan<- <note>Chen Houshan is the literary name of Chen Shidao (1053–1102), a prominent poet of the Song Dynasty known for his refined style.</note>

Fair hands pluck flowers, delicate and soft; emerging scents mingle together. Yet it requires the power of poetry and painting <term>danqing|Traditional Chinese painting, literally "red and blue-green" pigments</term> to capture this; I fear that a common hand would find it hard to dye the likeness.

A single glance makes one slightly enchanted; how can one bear to see it in person? I do not begrudge using my purple sleeves to brush away the clear dust, for I also wish to recognize the face of the spring wind.

### Lyric

-> Feng Yansi <-

<note>Feng Yansi (903–960) was a high-ranking official and a master of early lyric poetry during the Southern Tang dynasty. He is famous for his evocative, melancholic style.</note>

The wind suddenly rises, rippling the spring water of the entire pond. Leisurely, I guide the mandarin ducks <note>Mandarin ducks are traditional symbols of lovers and fidelity.</note> along the fragrant path, my hands rubbing the stamens of red apricot blossoms. ○

Alone, I lean against the railing by the duck-fighting pond. My green jade hairpin <term>sao-tou|Literally "head-scratcher," a poetic term for a hairpin</term> slips askew. All day long I wait for you, but you do not arrive. Raising my head, I hear... <note>The poem likely ends with "the cry of a magpie," a traditional omen of a guest's arrival or good news.</note>

### Zhu Xizhen
<note>Zhu Xizhen is the pseudonym of Zhu Dunru (1081–1159), a prominent lyricist of the Song Dynasty known for his transition from a carefree youth to a somber patriot following the fall of Northern China.</note>

> I was once accustomed to being detained by lovely blossoms, 
> Amidst dancing butterflies and the warbling of orioles. 
> In the playgrounds of youth and the blissful realm of wine, 
> I heedlessly let the spring slip away.
> 
> ○
> 
> Today, spring fades in the lands south of the River. 
> Where has my youthful face gone? 
> Do not compare these tangled sorrows to falling flowers: 
> The flowers are finite in number, but sorrow is infinite. 
> <note>The "River" (Jiangnan) refers to the region south of the Yangtze, a cultural heartland often associated with beauty and loss in Chinese poetry.</note>

<term>一落索 (Yi luo suo)</term> <note>Literally "The Fallen Rope" or "A String of Pearls," a specific pattern used for writing lyrics meant to be sung to a fixed melody.</note>

### Lyric Poem
<note>term: Lyric Poem (Ci) — a genre of Chinese poetry written to fit the melody of existing songs, characterized by lines of unequal length.</note>

->Huang Shangu<-
<note>Huang Shangu is the pseudonym of Huang Tingjian (1045–1105), a preeminent poet and calligrapher of the Northern Song Dynasty.</note>

Where has spring gone? 
Desolate, no path remains to follow. 
If anyone knows where spring has hidden, 
Pray, call it back to stay with us.

〇

Spring left no trace. 
Who knows where? 
None, unless one asks the golden oriole. 
Through a hundred warbles that no one can understand, 
It flies past the roses on the breeze.
<note>This poem is a classic of the "Pure Peaceful Music" (Qing Ping Yue) form, personifying the season of spring as a fleeting guest.</note>

<term>Huang Shangu: Huang Tingjian</term>
<term>Lyric (Ci): Song-poetry</term>
<term>Pure Peaceful Music: Qing Ping Yue (the name of the musical meter)</term>
<term>Oblique Rhyme (Ze yun): Rhymes using the rising, departing, or entering tones</term>
<term>Level Rhyme (Ping yun): Rhymes using the level tone</term>
<term>Rhyme Concordance (Ye): A term indicating that a line must rhyme with the preceding established rhyme</term>
<term>Stanza Break (Huan tou): Literally "changing the head," the beginning of the second stanza of a lyric</term>

<summary>This page illustrates the tonal and rhythmic structure of the "Pure Peaceful Music" lyric meter, followed by a famous example by Huang Tingjian about the search for the fleeting spring.</summary>
<keywords>Huang Tingjian, Qing Ping Yue, Pure Peaceful Music, Lyric Meter, Song Dynasty Poetry, Tonal Patterns, Prosody</keywords>

## The Water Clock at Night
<note>original: "Geng Lou Zi" (更漏子). The title refers to the "water clock" or clepsydra used to measure the watches of the night.</note>
<gloss>The first stanza has six lines, four rhymes, and twenty-three characters. The second stanza follows the same pattern.</gloss>

### Lyric Poem
<note>term: Lyric Poem (Ci) — a poetic form originally set to music, using "tune patterns" (meters) with fixed numbers of characters, tones, and rhymes.</note>

->Wen Feiqing<-
<note>Wen Feiqing is the courtesy name of Wen Tingyun (c. 812–866), a late Tang Dynasty poet famous for his ornate, sensual style and his foundational role in the development of the Lyric Poem genre.</note>

> Incense in the jade burner. Red wax tears. Shining askance on the painted hall, autumn thoughts. Eyebrow green fades. Temple hair like clouds disarrayed. The night is long, quilt and pillow cold.
>
> 〇 
>
> The wutong tree. The midnight rain. Not knowing that the pain of parting is at its bitterest. Leaf after leaf. Sound after sound. Dripping on the empty steps until dawn.
> <note>The "wutong" (Firmiana simplex) is the phoenix tree; its large leaves make a distinct sound in the rain, often symbolizing loneliness in Chinese poetry.</note>

<note>original: "Ruan Lang Gui" (阮郎歸). This meter is named after a legend of two men, Ruan Zhao and Liu Chen, who found a paradise of peach blossoms and immortal maidens, only to find centuries had passed when they returned home.</note>

<gloss>The first stanza has four lines, four rhymes, and twenty-four characters.</gloss>
<gloss>The second stanza has five lines, four rhymes, and twenty-three characters.</gloss>

# Spring in the Painted Hall
<note>original: "Hua Tang Chun" (畫堂春). The "Painted Hall" refers to an elegantly decorated reception hall or inner chamber in a traditional Chinese estate, often associated with the private lives of the gentry.</note>

<image-desc>
A metrical diagram for the song lyric pattern "Spring in the Painted Hall." The chart uses circular symbols to represent the tonal structure of the poem: empty circles (◯) for level tones, filled circles (●) for oblique tones, and half-filled circles (◐) for flexible tones where either is acceptable. 

**First stanza:** Four lines consisting of 7, 5, 7, and 5 characters. Annotations specify that it "starts with a level rhyme" and requires "rhyming with level tones."
**Second stanza:** Five lines consisting of 3, 3, 5, 7, and 5 characters. The labels identify the starting three-character line and the subsequent counts.
</image-desc>

->Lyric Poem<-

->Ouyang Yongshu<-
<note>Ouyang Yongshu is the courtesy name of Ouyang Xiu (1007–1072), one of the "Eight Great Masters of the Tang and Song." He was a pivotal figure in the Song Dynasty's literary and political life.</note>

In the southern garden, mid-spring, the time for "treading the green,"
<note>term: "Treading the Green" (踏青, taqing) — a traditional spring outing, often associated with the Qingming Festival, where people walk in the countryside to enjoy the new grass.</note>
The breeze is gentle, and I hear a horse's neigh.
Green plums are small as beans, willow leaves like eyebrows;
The day is long, and butterflies fly.
◯ <note>Metrical marker: Indicates a level tone is required here.</note> Heavy dew on the flowers, low mist over the grass;
Before the houses, curtains and screens hang low.
Wearied by the swing, she loosens her silk robes;
In the painted hall, a pair of swallows fly.

---

Second stanza: Five lines, three rhymes, twenty-three characters.

**Silk Robes:** Elegant clothing of the upper class.
**Painted Hall:** A decorated inner room.

# Song Lyric
<note>original: "Ci" (詞). This refers to the "Song Lyric" genre, which originated as lyrics written to existing popular melodies. By the Ming Dynasty, when this book was compiled, the music was largely lost, but the metrical patterns remained strictly codified.</note>

->Qin Shaoyou<-
<note>Qin Shaoyou is the courtesy name of Qin Guan (1049–1100), a renowned poet of the Northern Song Dynasty famous for his delicate and sentimental style. The tune pattern featured here is "Longing in Response to Heaven" (應天長, Ying Tian Chang).</note>

The east wind blows the willows as the days begin to lengthen; after the rain, fragrant grass glows in the setting sun. Apricot blossoms scatter and fall, making the swallows’ mud fragrant. Sleep has marred her rosy makeup. ○ <gloss>The incense coil silently burns away; on painted screens of phoenixes, the Xiao and Xiang rivers seem far away. The evening chill lightly pierces through her thin silk garments.</gloss>
<note>term: "Incense coil" (香篆, xiangzhuan) — incense powder burned in an intricate, seal-script pattern. As it burns, it acts as a clock. The "Xiao and Xiang" (瀟湘) refers to a region in Hunan famous in literature as a place of exile and longing.</note>
Boundless longing. <gloss>In this melody, the two stanzas each have one extra character in their final lines.</gloss>

<column-break/>

Rhyme: Twenty-three characters

<image-desc>A vertical schematic diagram outlining the tonal and rhythmic structure (meter) for the lyric pattern. It consists of eight columns of circles, representing the syllables. 

1. Seven circles: "First line, seven characters, beginning with a level rhyme."
2. Six circles: "Second line, six characters, rhyming in a level tone."
3. Seven circles: "Third line, seven characters, rhyming in a level tone."
4. Four circles: "Fourth line, four characters, rhyming in a level tone."
<note>The poem text on the left actually has five characters in this line ("Sleep has marred her rosy makeup"), matching the gloss about having "one extra character."</note>
5. Six circles: "Starting line of the second stanza, six characters."
6. Six circles: "Second line, six characters, rhyming in a level tone."
7. Seven circles: "Third line, seven characters, rhyming in a level tone."
8. Four circles: "Fourth line, four characters, rhyming in a level tone."
<note>Again, the text has five characters here ("Boundless longing"), following the variation noted in the gloss.</note></image-desc>

-> Zhang Ziye <-
<note>Zhang Ziye is the courtesy name of Zhang Xian (990–1078), a prominent Northern Song poet. He was famously nicknamed "Zhang of the Three Shadows" for his creative use of the word "shadow" in three distinct poems, one of which involves the "moon shadow" and "curtain shadow" seen here.</note>

As you came, dew dampened your robes, the fragrance turned moist; tresses of hair hung silk-fine by your temples. Raising the curtain, you rejoiced to see the moon so near; lifting your wine, you felt close to the flowers.
<note>original: "卷簾還喜月相親把酒與花相近" — These lines capture the intimate atmosphere typical of Zhang Xian's work, emphasizing the interplay between the human subject and the natural elements of the night.</note>

Going west past the Yang Pass—ask no more; before the song is sung, the sorrow is already felt. Beneath Jade Peak Mountain, the waters flow long—flowing on and on.
<note>term: "Yang Pass" (Yangguan) — a historical mountain pass in Gansu. In Chinese poetry, it is the quintessential symbol of parting and exile, as it was the final gateway to the desolate western frontier. "Jade Peak Mountain" (Yufeng Shan) likely refers to a specific peak near the site of the parting.</note>

## Song Lyric

-> Yan Tongshu <-
<note>Yan Tongshu is the courtesy name of Yan Shu (991–1055), a high-ranking official of the Northern Song Dynasty and a seminal figure in the development of the "Ci" poetic form. His style is characterized by a refined, "gentlemanly" melancholy.</note>

Yesterday, I went seeking news of the spring; <note>original: "Tan chun xiaoxi" (探春消息) — a poetic way of looking for the first buds or signs of the season.</note>
On the lake, the green waves were calm and level.
Helplessly, the fragrant grass winding around the embankment
Still grows according to its old traces. <note>This suggests that while the season has returned, the cyclical nature of nature reminds the poet of things past or lost.</note>

## Song Lyric <gloss>Watching snow on Lantern Night, after which the moon shone bright again</gloss>

Mao Zemin <note>Mao Zemin is the courtesy name of Mao Pang (c. 1060–1124), a Song Dynasty official and poet celebrated for the refined and delicate nature of his lyrics.</note>

The wind passes through icy eaves, tinkling like jade pendants; <note>original: "huan pei" (環珮) — a metaphor comparing the sound of ice to the clinking of jade ornaments.</note>
Mist from the night lingers upon the ornate carpets.
Scattered "jade flowers" <term>Jade flowers (yaohua): A poetic and elegant term for snowflakes.</term> reflect the moonlight;
One only fears the presence of a single speck of dust.

〇

The golden royal lamps in the mouths of phoenixes whirl and dazzle;
In a drunken state, one feels the cold grow light.
If only this clear light could shine brightly upon us,
We would not fail this spring that reaches the fifth watch of the night. <note>The "fifth watch" refers to the final period of the night, just before dawn.</note>

# Begonia Spring <note>original: "Haitang Chun" (海棠春). The Haitang is the Chinese Flowering Crabapple, often called "Begonia" in English translations, a symbol of feminine beauty and the fragility of spring.</note>

### Song Lyric <gloss>Spring Dawn</gloss> Poetry Remnant <term>Poetry Remnant (Shiyu): A traditional alternative name for the "Ci" (song lyric) genre, suggesting it was a creative evolution or "leftover" of classical Shi poetry.</term>

Warbling orioles sing skillfully outside the window;
Before I have had enough sleep, they startle me awake.
The morning chill is light against the kingfisher-green quilt;
From the ornate seal-script burner, the incense smoke curls and fades. <note>original: "bao zhuan" (寶篆) — refers to incense coils or burners designed in the shape of archaic seal-script characters.</note>

The lingering intoxication from last night has not yet dissolved.
The palace maids report that in the other courtyard,
The gathering for music and song has already begun.
Let me ask: of the begonia flowers,
How many blossomed last night?

## Lyric: Late Spring <note>original: "Ci Chunmu" (詞春暮). While the heading identifies the theme as "Late Spring," the poem itself follows the "Ripples Sifting Sand" (Lang Tao Sha) pattern.</note>
->Li Houzhu <note>original: "Li Houzhu" (李後主), literally "The Last Ruler Li." This refers to Li Yu (c. 937–978), the last emperor of the Southern Tang dynasty and a master of the Ci poetic form.</note><-

> Outside the curtain, the rain patters on; the spirit of spring is fading. My silk quilt offers no protection against the chill of the fifth watch <term>The fifth watch (Wugeng): The period before dawn, roughly 3:00 AM to 5:00 AM.</term>. In my dream, I did not know I was a guest <note>original: "ke" (客) — here meaning "an exile" or "a prisoner," reflecting Li Yu's status after his kingdom was conquered.</note>; for a brief moment, I indulged in joy. ○ Do not lean against the railing alone; the boundless rivers and mountains are gone. Leaving them was easy, but seeing them again is difficult. Flowing water and falling blossoms—spring has departed; it is the difference between heaven and the world of men.

# Collected Illustrations of the Three Realms: Literature and History, Volume 3
<meta>Catchword: Level Nine</meta>

# Lyric: Boudoir Sentiments <note>A common theme in "Ci" poetry focusing on the inner lives, longings, and seasonal reflections of women in their private quarters.</note>
->Zhao Delin <note>Zhao Lingzhi (1061–1134), a member of the Song imperial family known for his expertise in music and poetry.</note><-

Up in the tower, delicate willow catkins wind around the curtains. At the wall's edge, low-hanging blossoms obstruct the moon. Year after year, the affairs of spring fade away. Heartfelt matters cause such grief, like crows seeking a place to perch. ○ The "mirror-dancing" phoenix <note>A reference to a legend where a phoenix only dances when it sees its own reflection; here, it signifies loneliness.</note> leaves behind a trace of kingfisher green. Tear-pearls diminish as the red phoenix candle leans sideways. Heavy doors cannot lock away the dreams of longing; they follow my whims to the very ends of the earth.

### Chao Zhong Cuo <note>original: "Chao Zhong Cuo" (朝中措). A "Ci" melody title, often translated as "Gathering in Court" or "The Courtier's Custom."</note>
<gloss>The first stanza consists of four lines and two rhymes, totaling twenty-four characters. The second stanza consists of five lines and two rhymes, totaling twenty-four characters.</gloss>

# Lyric: Pingshan Hall
->### Ouyang Yongshu <note>Ouyang Xiu (1007–1072), a towering figure in Song Dynasty literature and politics. He built Pingshan Hall in Yangzhou, which became a famous site for literary gatherings.</note><-

At Pingshan Hall, the railings lean against the clear sky; mountain colors appear and disappear in the mist. The weeping willows I planted with my own hands before the hall—how many times has the spring wind blown since I left? 〇 The Prefect of Literature <note>A self-reference to Ouyang Xiu, who served as the prefectural governor.</note> wields his brush for ten thousand words; drinking a thousand cups in one sitting. One must seek joy while young; look at the withered old man before the wine vessel. <note>Ouyang Xiu reflects on his aging self compared to his younger, more vibrant days at the hall.</note>

---

The first stanza consists of five lines and three rhymes, totaling twenty-four characters. The second stanza is the same as the first. <gloss>The tones for the opening line differ between variations.</gloss>

# Lyric Poetry <note>original: "Ci" (詞). A genre of Chinese poetry written to be sung to existing melodies.</note>
## Poetry Remnants <note>original: "Shi-yu" (詩餘). A traditional alternative name for *Ci* poetry, literally meaning the "remnants" or "overflow" of the formal *Shi* poetic tradition.</note>

At dusk in the tower, the apricot blossoms are cold;
A slanted moon hangs over the small railing.
A pair of swallows; two rows of returning geese;
The sound of the painted horn <note>A military or ceremonial horn often used in poetry to signal the end of the day or a sense of melancholy.</note> fades away.
○
A person by the patterned window, in the midst of the east wind;
Wordless, facing the idle spring.
It should still be as it was of old:
Shimmering autumn waters and faint spring mountains. <note>These are classic metaphors for a beautiful woman's features: "autumn waters" refers to clear, bright eyes, and "spring mountains" refers to the soft, arched shape of eyebrows.</note>

---

## Spring in the Grotto-Heaven <note>original: "Dong Tian Chun" (洞天春). A "cipai" or melody title. "Grotto-heavens" are sacred sites in Taoism.</note>

The first stanza consists of four lines and four rhymes, totaling twenty-four characters. The second stanza has five lines and three
-> rhymes, totaling twenty-four characters. <-

<image-desc>
A metrical chart for the "Spring in the Grotto-Heaven" melody. It uses vertical columns to define the structure. Each column specifies the line number, character count, and rhyme requirements, followed by a series of circles representing the required tonal pattern. Empty circles (○) indicate level tones, filled circles (●) indicate oblique tones, and partially filled circles indicate flexible tones.
</image-desc>

The first line has six characters, beginning with an oblique rhyme <note>original: "ze yun" (仄韻). In Chinese prosody, tones are categorized as "Level" (ping) or "Oblique" (ze) to create rhythm.</note>.
The second line has six characters, rhyming in the oblique tone <note>original: "xie" (叶), meaning to rhyme or harmonize with the previous rhyme sounds.</note>.
The third line has seven characters, rhyming in the oblique tone.
The fourth line has five characters, rhyming in the oblique tone.
The opening line [of the second stanza] has six characters, rhyming in the oblique tone.
The second line has six characters, rhyming in the oblique tone.
The third line has four characters.
The fourth line has five characters.
Rhyme in the oblique tone.

<note>Glossary of technical terms from the original text: Lyric (詞), Poetry Remnants (詩餘), Spring in the Grotto-Heaven (洞天春), Oblique Rhyme (仄韻), To Rhyme (仄叶), Level and Oblique Tones (平仄), Melody Title (詞牌).</note>

# Lyric Poetry <note>original: "Ci" (詞). A form of Classical Chinese poetry intended to be sung to specific, pre-existing melodies known as *cipai*.</note>

-> Ouyang Yongshu <- <note>The courtesy name of Ouyang Xiu (1007–1072), a towering figure of the Song Dynasty known for his essays, history, and poetry.</note>

> Orioles warble where the green leaves are thick, their voices early;
> Outside the threshold, the fallen red petals are not yet swept.
> Dewdrops like pearls spread through the fragrant grass;
> Just then, behind the curtains, comes the clear dawn.
> ○
> The courtyard with the swing is quiet and still;
> Once again, the Qingming <note>The Tomb-Sweeping Festival, held in early spring, often associated in poetry with the passing of the season and a sense of melancholy.</note> festival has passed.
> Swallows and butterflies are flighty and wild, willow tendrils are tangled;
> How much spring longing is stirred?

---

-> Lyric Poetry <-

-> Yan Shuyuan <- <note>The courtesy name of Yan Jidao (c. 1038–1110), a Song Dynasty poet famous for his "Ci" poems that often dwell on themes of lost love and nostalgic longing.</note>

> In other gardens, the shade fades as I prepare to return,
> Just as the season of spring is being escorted away.
> The person in my eyes has departed, making joy hard to find;
> With whom can I share this single, fragrant chalice? <note>original: "Jia" (斝). An ancient Chinese ritual wine vessel, often used in poetry to evoke antiquity and elegance.</note>
> ○
> The red railings and emerald steps <note>original: "Biqi" (碧砌). Decorative stone stairs, often described as 'emerald' due to moss or the color of the stone, symbolizing the enduring nature of a place compared to the fleeting nature of human presence.</note> are all as they were;
> I remember when we held hands.
> If there is love, it matters not how long the separation lasts;
> So long as love remains, a reunion will eventually come.

# Lyrics <note>original: "Ci" (詞). A form of Classical Chinese poetry consisting of lyrics written to specific musical melodies, characterized by lines of unequal length.</note>

## Ye Daoqing <note>The courtesy name of Ye Mengde (1077–1148), a high-ranking Southern Song official and celebrated poet known for his elegant and often melancholic lyrics.</note>

Fill the cup with green wine <note>original: "Luxu" (綠醑). A poetic term for fine, freshly brewed wine, often described as having a green tint or "ants" (foam) on the surface.</note> to make you stay; do not depart in such haste. Of the scene, one part is spring color, one part is sorrow and gloom, and one part is wind and rain. ○ The flowers bloom and the flowers fade—they all come to an end. Just sing loudly and do not stop. Who knows where we might meet again during the peony season next year? <gloss>Note: This melody has many variations. Here I have only taken the version from the *Poetry Remnants* <note>original: "Shiyu" (詩餘). A common term for Ci poetry, implying it is the "leftover" or extension of traditional Shi poetry.</note> as the standard. The opening line of the second stanza was originally seven characters; because of the character "how many" (ji), it rhymes. Because one character was missing, the text was incomplete and redundant.</gloss>

---

### Willow Tip Green <note>original: "Liu Shao Qing" (柳梢青). A melody name or *cipai*. In Ci poetry, the title provides the rhythmic template rather than the subject matter.</note>

-> The first stanza has six lines, three rhymes, and twenty-four characters. The second stanza has five lines, three rhymes, and twenty-five characters. <-

**Metrical Guide:**

| Line | Character Count | Tone and Rhyme Requirement |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **First Stanza** | | |
| Line 1 | 4 characters | ○ ● ● ○ (Level rhyme) |
| Line 2 | 3 characters | ● ○ ● |
| Line 3 | 3 characters | ● ● ○ |
| Line 4 | 2 characters | ● ○ |
| Line 5 | 4 characters | ○ ● ● ○ |
| Line 6 | 4 characters | ● ● ○ ○ |
| Line 7 | 4 characters | ● ● ○ ○ (Level rhyme leaf) <note>original: "Xie" (叶). Indicates the line must harmonize with the previous rhyme.</note> |
| **Second Stanza** | | |

| Line 5 | 5 characters | ● ● ○ ○ ○ (Level rhyme leaf) |

<note>In these diagrams, circles (○) represent "level" tones (ping) and dots (●) represent "oblique" tones (ze). This was a rigorous system used by poets to ensure the lyrics fit the musical melody perfectly.</note>

# Lyrics <note>original: "Ci" (詞). Poetry written to be sung to existing popular melodies, featuring lines of varying lengths.</note>
->Poetic Remnants<- <note>original: "Shiyu" (詩餘). A traditional name for the Lyric genre, suggesting it is a beautiful development arising from the "remnants" of classical Shi poetry.</note>

Grassy banks and level sands. The ancient garden of the King of Wu <note>A reference to the historical sites of the Kingdom of Wu, centered in modern-day Suzhou, evoking a sense of lost grandeur.</note>. Willows sway as the mist slants. After the rain, the chill is light. Before the wind, the fragrance is soft. Spring resides in the pear blossoms. 〇 A traveler’s single oar at the edge of the sky. In the place where I wake from wine, the setting sun shines on scattered crows. A swing stands outside the gate. Rouge and powder <note>A metonymy for beautiful women.</note> are glimpsed atop the wall. Within the deep courtyard—whose house is this?

---

# Lyrics <note>original: "Ci" (詞). A genre of Chinese poetry written to be sung to existing popular melodies, characterized by lines of unequal length and strict tonal patterns.</note>

->By Yan Shuyuan<- <note>The courtesy name of Yan Jidao (c. 1038–1110), a famous Song dynasty poet known for his bittersweet lyrics about love and memory.</note>

By the green balustrades, the twilight moon is faint. Hand in hand, we face the fading red blossoms. Behind the shadows of the gauze window <note>A window covered with thin silk, often used in poetry to suggest a refined, intimate domestic setting.</note>, we are hazy with spring slumber. Apricot blossoms on a small folding screen. 〇 One must worry that after we part, the sky will be high and the sea will be vast—where will we meet again? Fortunately, there is this cup of fragrant wine before the flowers; do not be in such a hurry to plan your return.

---

## Mulan Flowers with Stolen Tones <note>original: "Tousheng Mulan Hua" (偷聲木蘭花). A melody name or "cipai." The term "stolen tones" refers to a rhythmic variation where certain beats of the standard "Mulan Flowers" melody are omitted or shortened, creating a faster tempo.</note>
The first stanza has four lines, four rhymes, and twenty-five characters. The second stanza has four...

<image-desc>A metrical diagram for the 'Mulan Flowers with Stolen Tones' melody. It features vertical columns of circles: open circles (○) represent "level" tones (ping), which are long and steady; filled circles (●) represent "deflected" tones (ze), which are shorter and more rhythmic. Annotations next to the columns dictate the rhyme scheme.</image-desc>

○ ○ ● ● ○ ○ ● <gloss>First line: seven characters, beginning with a deflected rhyme <note>original: "ze yun" (仄韻). A rhyme using the clipped or rising tones of Middle Chinese.</note></gloss>
○ ○ ● ● ○ ○ ● <gloss>Second line: seven characters, harmonized deflected rhyme <note>original: "ze xie" (仄叶). This indicates the line must rhyme with the preceding deflected-tone rhyme.</note></gloss>
● ● ○ ○ <gloss>Third line: four characters, changing to a level rhyme <note>original: "ping yun" (平韻). A change in the melody where the rhyme shifts to a steady, level tone.</note></gloss>
● ● ○ ○ ○ ○ ● <gloss>Fourth line: seven characters, harmonized level rhyme <note>original: "ping xie" (平叶).</note></gloss>

### Dripping Gold
<gloss>The first stanza consists of five lines, four rhymes, and twenty-five characters. The second stanza follows the same pattern.</gloss>

<note>The following chart uses circles to indicate the required tones for each syllable. White circles (〇) represent "level" (ping) tones, while black circles (●) represent "oblique" (ze) tones—the two fundamental categories of sound in classical Chinese poetic prosody.</note>

〇 〇 ● ● ● ● ● <gloss>First line: seven characters, beginning with an oblique rhyme.</gloss>
● 〇 〇 <gloss>Second line: three characters.</gloss>
● 〇 〇 <gloss>Third line: three characters, rhyming in the oblique tone.</gloss>

〇 〇 〇 ● ● <gloss>Fifth line: five characters, rhyming in the oblique tone.</gloss>

<note>This second chart illustrates a variation or a different rhythmic structure, possibly an opening sequence for a related melody.</note>

〇 〇 ● ● ● ● ● <gloss>Opening line: seven characters, changing to an oblique rhyme.</gloss>
● ● 〇 〇 ● ● 〇 <gloss>Second line: seven characters, rhyming in the oblique tone.</gloss>
● 〇 〇 <gloss>Third line.</gloss>
● ● 〇 〇 〇 ● 〇 <gloss>Fourth line: seven characters, rhyming in the level tone.</gloss>

# Lyrics
## Zhang Ziye
<note>Zhang Xian (990–1078), courtesy name Ziye, was a prominent poet of the Northern Song Dynasty. He was nicknamed "Zhang of the Three Shadows" for his famous descriptions of shadows in his poetry.</note>

Snow cages the jade garden, and the plum blossoms are lean;
Beyond the outer courtyard's layered gates, the precious beast door-knockers are joined.
A moon from the sea, newly born, has climbed the high tower.
Helpless is this emotion 〇 <note>The circle here indicates a rhythmic beat or a missing character in the original template.</note>
The waves of the curtain do not stir; the silver lamp burns low.
This night, the night is long—how can one endure it?
Longing for a dream of Gaotang, <note>Gaotang (高唐) refers to a legendary site where a King met a goddess in a dream; it is a classical literary metaphor for a romantic or sexual encounter.</note>
I only fear that upon waking, my heartbreak will be all the more bitter.

->Lyric Poetry<-

->Mao Zemin<- <note>A poet of the Song Dynasty (960–1279), known for his contributions to the lyric genre.</note>

Tears wet the balustrade like dew clinging to flowers.
Grief gathers at the brow like emerald mountain peaks.
This resentment is shared equally between us.
With no more words to say,
We only stare at each other in vain.
◯
Scattered rain and broken clouds <note>A metaphor for a disrupted or fading romance.</note> leave the mind listless.
Lonely, morning after morning, evening after evening.
Tonight, deep in the mountains...

### Parting Sorrows
<note>original: "Xi Fen Fei" (惜分飛). This title literally means "Grieving the Divided Flight," a common metaphor for the separation of a couple.</note>

The first stanza consists of four lines, four rhymes, and twenty-five characters. The second stanza follows the same pattern.

<image-desc>A metrical diagram for the "Parting Sorrows" tune pattern. It consists of four columns of circles representing the tone requirements for each character. Filled circles (●) represent "oblique" (ze) tones, empty circles (○) represent "level" (ping) tones, and circles with a dot (⦿) indicate where a rhyme must occur.</image-desc>

<margin>First line: seven characters, beginning with an oblique rhyme <note>original: "ze yun" (仄韻). In classical Chinese prosody, oblique tones provide a more abrupt or forceful sound compared to level tones.</note></margin>
●●○○●●⦿

<margin>Second line: six characters, rhyming in the oblique tone <note>original: "xie" (叶). This term indicates that the syllable must harmonize or rhyme with the preceding rhyme points.</note></margin>
●●●○○⦿

<margin>Third line: five characters, rhyming in the oblique tone</margin>
○○●●⦿

<margin>Fourth line: seven characters, rhyming in the oblique tone</margin>
●●○○●●⦿

<column-break/>

The Qin pavilion <note>original: "Qin Lou" (秦樓). A poetic reference to a lady’s chamber or a place of romance.</note> deeply locks in the unfaithful lover.
Through the long, lingering night, with whom can it be shared?
Ashamed to see the mandarin ducks and phoenixes <note>original: "Yuan Feng" (鴛鳳). These birds are traditional symbols of marital bliss and fidelity; seeing them on the bedding emphasizes the speaker's loneliness.</note> on the pillows and quilts.
In moments of boredom, I simply huddle in my clothes.
◯
For no reason, the painted horns <note>Military horns used to signal time or alarms in a city.</note> of the fortified city stir.
Startling me out of a fresh dream.
Outside the window, the moonlight is heavy as frost.
I listen until the end of the "Plum Blossom Melodies" <note>original: "Meihua Nong" (梅花弄). A famous and melancholic musical composition for the flute or zither.</note>.

<vocab>
**Parting Sorrows** (Xi Fen Fei): The name of the musical melody to which these poems are set.
**Mao Zemin**: The author of the first lyric.
**Lyric Poetry** (Ci): A genre of poetry written to be sung to existing melodies.
**Oblique Rhyme** (Ze Yun): A rhyme scheme using tones that are not "level" (flat/prolonged).
**Rhyme/Harmonize** (Xie): To follow the established rhyme of the poem.
**Qin Pavilion**: A literary shorthand for a woman's quarters.
**Mandarin Ducks and Phoenixes**: Symbols of a couple in love.
</vocab>

# The Fisherman’s Song
<note>original: "Yu Ge Zi" (漁歌子). This tune title traditionally evokes the image of a serene fisherman, a symbol of the scholar-official’s desire to escape the burdens of political life and return to nature.</note>

->The first stanza consists of six lines, four rhymes, and twenty-five characters. The second stanza follows the same pattern.<-

First line: three characters ○○● <note>○ represents a level tone; ● represents an oblique tone.</note>
Second line: three characters <gloss>Begins without rhyming</gloss> ●●●
Third line: seven characters <gloss>Rhyming in the "Hai" category</gloss> ○○●●●○○ <note>original: "Hai ye" (亥叶). This indicates that the line must end with a word from a specific rhyme group in the rhyme tables.</note>
Fourth line: three characters ●○●
Fifth line: three characters ●○●
Sixth line: six characters <gloss>Rhyming in the "Hai" category</gloss> ○●○○●●

### Singing of the Fisherman’s Home

->Li Xun<- <note>A poet of the Five Dynasties and Ten Kingdoms period (907–960). Interestingly, Li Xun was of Persian descent, his ancestors having settled in China to trade in incense and medicine. He became a master of the Chinese lyric form.</note>

The Chu mountains are green, the Xiang waters are emerald.
The spring breeze is gentle and vast; one can never see enough.
The grass is lush and thick, the flowers grow in clusters.
From the fishing boats, the rowing songs follow one after another.
◯
Drifting along freely, without any restraint.
Returning from fishing, riding the moonlight back to the curving bay.
Wine fills the goblet, clouds fill the hut.
One sees nothing of the glory or shame of the human world.

# The Twenty-Six Character Pattern
<note>original: "Ti Er Shi Liu Zi" (體二十六字). This refers to a specific structural template for a short lyric poem consisting of exactly twenty-six characters per stanza or section, as illustrated in the diagram below.</note>

<note>The following diagram serves as a metrical "blueprint" for poets. Circles represent the musical tones required for each syllable to ensure the poem could be sung correctly to a specific melody.</note>

| Line | Pattern and Instructions |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Line 1** | ● ○ ◑ ● ● ○ ○ <gloss>First line, seven characters, starting with a level rhyme</gloss> <note>Level tones (○) are long and flat; oblique tones (●) are short or changing. "◑" indicates a syllable where either tone is acceptable.</note> |
| **Line 2** | ● ● ○ ○ ● ● <gloss>Second line, six characters, level tone</gloss> |
| **Line 3** | ● ● ○ ○ ● ● ○ <gloss>Third line, seven characters</gloss> |
| **Line 4** | ● ● ○ ○ ● ● <gloss>Fourth line, six characters, rhyming in a level tone</gloss> <note>original: "Ping xie" (平叶). This indicates the line must conclude with a rhyme word from the "level" tone category to match the preceding rhymes.</note> |

->Lyric Poetry<-
<note>original: "Ci" (詞). A genre of Chinese poetry written to fit existing musical melodies, often characterized by lines of unequal length.</note>

->Liu Qiqing<-
<note>Liu Qiqing (c. 987–1053), better known as Liu Yong, was a revolutionary figure in the Song dynasty. He was famous for his "slow lyrics" and his popularity among the common people; it was said that "wherever there is a well, there are people singing Liu Yong's lyrics."</note>

Weaving silk and tailoring verses, the expression of my intent is deep.
It is truly worth a thousand pieces of gold.
Each time I unfurl and admire it, I chant with a sorrowful air.
My heart is tied in knots, and tears fill the folds of my robe.
Our secret joys have already scattered; our past promises are now distant.
Listless and lonely is my state today.
On fine stationery, I write much to send you my "fragrant message." <note>original: "Fang yin" (芳音). A poetic term for a letter or news from a beloved woman.</note>
I only fear that my heart will fall back into its old, longing state.

<note>The poem provided here is actually a version of the tune "Trimming the Silver Lamp" (Ti Yin Deng), which matches the themes of longing and delicate emotion typical of Liu Yong's style.</note>

<keywords>Liu Yong, Ci lyric poetry, metrical patterns, level and oblique tones, Northern Song Dynasty literature, romantic longing</keywords>

# Prelude to the Court of Imperial Sacrifices
<note>original: "Taichang Yin" (太常引). This is a melody title (cipai) to which poets composed lyrics. "Taichang" refers to the Court of Imperial Sacrifices, suggesting the melody may have originated in ceremonial or courtly music.</note>

**First Section:** Four lines, four rhymes, twenty-five characters.
**Second Section:** Five lines, three rhymes, twenty-five characters.

### Prosody Chart
<note>This diagram acts as a template for poets. The circles indicate the required musical tones for each syllable to ensure the poem harmonizes with the melody. Hollow circles (○) represent "level" tones, solid circles (●) represent "oblique" tones, and half-filled circles (◐) indicate flexibility.</note>

| Stanza | Line | Metrical Pattern | Instructions |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **First Stanza** | Line 1 | ● ○ ◑ ● ● ○ ○ | Seven characters; start with a level rhyme. |

| | Line 3 | ◑ ● ● ○ ○ | Five characters. |
| | Line 4 | ◑ ● ● ○ ○ | Five characters; rhyming in a level tone. <gloss>level rhyme</gloss> |
| **Second Stanza** | Line 1 | ◑ ○ ◑ ● ● ○ ○ | Seven characters. |

| | Line 3 | ◑ ● ● ○ ○ | Five characters. |
| | Line 4 | ◑ ● ● ○ ○ | Five characters. |

->Selected Lyric Patterns<-

->By Liu Jingxiu<-
<note>Liu Jingxiu (1249–1293), also known as Liu Yin, was a renowned scholar and poet of the late Song and early Yuan dynasties. He was famous for his integrity and his refusal to serve the Mongol court, choosing instead a life of reclusive study.</note>

A man’s career and merits have been hard to win since ancient times.
The world of men deceives us in thousands of ways.
Awakening from the dream of Handan,
<note>The "Dream of Handan" (or the Yellow Millet Dream) is a famous literary trope. A young man dreams of a whole lifetime of official success, only to wake up and find that the pot of millet being cooked for his lunch is not yet done—realizing that worldly glory is fleeting and illusory.</note>
It is better to watch the floating clouds pass by unhurriedly.

Where the red dust ends,
<note>"Red dust" is a traditional metaphor for the bustling, chaotic, and materialistic world of human society.</note>
Deep within the piles of white clouds,
Lying in reclusive peace, facing the green mountains.
In spirit and character, I am a companion to Chen Tuan.
<note>Chen Tuan (c. 871–989) was a legendary Taoist sage and hermit who lived on Mount Hua. He was famous for his mastery of internal alchemy and his ability to sleep for months at a time, symbolizing a state of deep meditative detachment from the world.</note>

<summary>This page details the structural requirements for the "Taichang Yin" lyric form, providing a tonal chart and a sample poem by Liu Yin. The poem reflects themes of reclusiveness and the futility of worldly ambition, typical of late Song and early Yuan intellectual culture.</summary>

<keywords>Taichang Yin, lyric poetry, prosody, Liu Yin, Handan Dream, Chen Tuan, Taoism, reclusiveness, Sancai Tuhui</keywords>

->**Poetic Remnants**<-
<note>original: "Shi-yu" (詩餘). This term literally means "remnants of poetry," a traditional name for the Ci genre, reflecting its origin as a more flexible evolution of the strictly regulated poetry of the Tang dynasty.</note>

->**Lyric Poetry**<-
<note>original: "Ci" (詞).</note>

On the green willow branches, the dawn warbler’s cry announces the mild and harmonious weather. A few notes are blown through the gauze window, startling me once more...

<column-break/>

...Do not mistakenly compare me to Xie An of years past. <note>Xie An (320–385 CE) was a legendary statesman of the Eastern Jin dynasty. He is a cultural icon of "coolness" and refinement, famously playing a game of go while receiving news of a massive military victory. The poet here suggests a humble or perhaps ironic distance from such a high historical standard.</note>

# Seeking Spring Melody
<note>original: "Tanchunling" (探春令). A melody name or "tune pattern" (cipai). Poets would write new lyrics to fit this specific musical and metrical blueprint.</note>

**First Section:** Four lines, two rhymes, twenty-six characters.
**Second Section:** Four lines, three rhymes, twenty-six characters.

### Prosody for Twenty-six Characters
<note>This diagram serves as a template for the poet. The circles indicate the required musical tones for each syllable. Hollow circles (○) represent "level" tones (ping), solid circles (●) represent "oblique" tones (ze), and half-filled circles (◐) indicate the tone is optional.</note>

| Stanza | Line | Tone Pattern | Instructions |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **First Stanza** | Line 1 | ◐ ○ ○ ◐ ● ○ ● | <gloss>First line: seven characters.</gloss> |
| | Line 2 | ◐ ● ● ○ ○ | <gloss>Second line: five characters; begins with an oblique rhyme.</gloss> |
| | Line 3 | ○ ○ ◐ ● ● ○ ○ ● | <gloss>Third line: eight characters.</gloss> |
| | Line 4 | ● ● ○ ○ ● ● | <gloss>Fourth line: six characters; rhyming in an oblique tone.</gloss> <note>original: "ze-ye" (仄叶). This indicates that the rhyme must belong to the "oblique" category of tones to harmonize with the melody.</note> |
| **Second Stanza** | Line 1 | ● ○ ○ ● ● ○ ● | <gloss>Opening line: seven characters; rhyming in an oblique tone.</gloss> |
| | Line 2 | ◐ ● ● ○ ● | <gloss>Second line: five characters; rhyming in an oblique tone.</gloss> |
| | Line 3 | ○ ○ ◐ ● ● ○ ○ ● | <gloss>Third line: eight characters.</gloss> |
| | Line 4 | ● ● ○ ○ ● ● | <gloss>Fourth line: six characters; rhyming in an oblique tone.</gloss> |

<note>Vocabulary used in this section: 
- Seeking Spring Melody (探春令): The tune title.
- Lyric Poetry (詞): The literary genre.
- Poetic Remnants (詩餘): An alternative name for lyrics.
- Rhyme (韻): The phonetic harmony.
- Oblique Rhyme (仄韻/仄叶): Rhyming on a non-level tone.
- Level and Oblique (平仄): The two primary tonal categories in Chinese prosody.
- Tune Manual (詞譜): The formal collection of these patterns.</note>

The beautiful lady awakens from sleep. Her hair, like thick dark clouds, hangs slanted and disheveled, and her golden hairpin has fallen out. It is as if her fragrant heart is intoxicated. For her young lover, she has soaked her handkerchief of fine shark-silk; it is entirely covered with tears of longing.
<note>original: "Shark-silk" (jiaoxiao 鮫綃). In Chinese mythology, this refers to a waterproof silk woven by merfolk (sharks). In poetry, it is a luxurious metaphor for a fine silk handkerchief, often associated with weeping.</note>

Yesterday it was warm, yet suddenly it is cold again; the wind and rain only settled late in the evening. The courtyard and veranda are lonely. As the Pure Brightness festival approaches, I drink wine among the withered blossoms.
<note>original: "Pure Brightness" (Qingming 清明). A traditional Chinese festival for honoring ancestors, falling in early spring, often associated with rain, cold spells, and a sense of melancholy.</note>
It is that same illness from last year. <gloss>○</gloss> From the top of the tower, the sound of the painted horn is blown by the wind, waking me. As night falls, the heavy gates are silent. How can I bear it? Once more, the bright moon sends the shadow of a swing over the wall.
<note>The "painted horn" (huajiao 畵角) was a wooden instrument decorated with patterns, used in military watches or to signal the time in ancient Chinese cities.</note>

### Lyric

->By Zhang Ziye<-
<note>Zhang Ziye (張子野) is the courtesy name of Zhang Xian (990–1078), a celebrated poet of the Northern Song Dynasty famous for his "three shadows"—three specific poetic lines involving shadows.</note>

Neither the jasper forest nor the jade trees can compare to her. Clothing thin as clouds; a waist slender as a willow. With just one style of dress, she possesses a hundred kinds of charm. Her eyebrows and eyes are elegant, perfectly shaped as if they were painted. <gloss>○</gloss> The east wind ruffles the grass as various flowers drift; I regret there is no way to climb the green branches, and so we begin to sing a duet once more.

# Shadow of the Drunk Flower
<note>original: "Zui Hua Yin" (醉花陰). A "Ci" (lyric) tune pattern. The name evokes a sense of intoxication amidst garden blossoms.</note>
<gloss>The first stanza has four lines, three rhymes, and twenty-six characters. The second stanza begins with the same line structure as the first,</gloss>
<gloss>but the level and oblique tones of the opening line are different.</gloss>

<image-desc>A tonal pattern diagram for the lyric tune "Shadow of the Drunk Flower." Tones are indicated by circular symbols: White circles (○) for level tones, black circles (●) for oblique tones, and half-filled circles (◐) for variable tones. The requirements are as follows:
1. First line (7 symbols): ◐ ● ○ ○ ○ ● ●
2. Second line (5 symbols): ○ ○ ● ● ●
3. Third line (5 symbols): ○ ○ ● ● ●
4. Fourth line (9 symbols): ◐ ● ○ ○ ◐ ● ● ● ●
Each line specifies character counts and rhyming constraints.</image-desc>

<gloss>The first line has seven characters and does not rhyme at the start.</gloss>
<gloss>The second line has five characters.</gloss>
<gloss>The third line has five characters.</gloss>
<gloss>The fourth line has nine characters, rhyming in the 'hai' category.</gloss>
<note>Traditional Chinese poetry is categorized by rhyme groups. The "hai" (亥) category refers to a specific group of sounds that must match for the poem to be musically harmonious.</note>

### Lyric Poem
**By Li Yi'an**
<note>Li Yi'an is the pseudonym of Li Qingzhao (1084–c. 1155), the most famous female poet of the Song Dynasty. This specific poem is one of the most celebrated works in the Chinese canon.</note>

Thin mist and thick clouds, I sorrow over the long, endless day;
Auspicious incense smoke puffs from the golden beast burner.
The festive season of the Double Ninth arrives again;
<note>The Double Ninth Festival (Chongyang 重陽) is held on the 9th day of the 9th lunar month. It is a time of autumn reflection, often associated with drinking chrysanthemum wine and longing for absent loved ones.</note>
Through the jade pillow and gauze curtains, the midnight autumn chill seeps.

○

By the eastern hedge, I hold my wine after dusk;
<note>The "eastern hedge" is a classic literary allusion to the poet Tao Yuanming, signifying a place of quiet reflection and the cultivation of chrysanthemums.</note>
A subtle fragrance fills my sleeves.
Do not say that my soul is not consumed;
The curtain rolls up in the west wind; I am thinner than the yellow chrysanthemum.
<note>The "yellow flower" (huanghua 黃花) refers to the chrysanthemum. This famous closing line compares the poet’s physical wasting—caused by the sorrow of separation—to the delicate, slender petals of the autumn flower.</note>

<summary>This page outlines the metrical structure of the song pattern "Shadow of the Drunk Flower" and provides its most famous example: a poem by Li Qingzhao expressing autumn melancholy.</summary>
<keywords>Li Qingzhao, Zui Hua Yin, Ci poetry, Double Ninth Festival, tonal patterns, Song Dynasty literature, Sancai Tuhui</keywords>

# The Southern Branch <gloss>original: "Nan Ke Zi" (南柯子); also known as "Song of the South" (Nan Ge Zi)</gloss>

The first stanza consists of four lines, three rhymes, and twenty-six characters. The second stanza follows the same structure as the first.

->Lyric Poem<-
->**Su Dongpo**<-
<note>Su Dongpo (1037–1101), also known as Su Shi, was a giant of Song Dynasty literature. He was a statesman, calligrapher, and poet known for his bold and expressive style.</note>

The mountains are like the furrowed brows of a singer; the waves flow like a gaze from emerald eyes. The sightseers have all climbed the Twelve Towers <note>A poetic reference to high, scenic buildings or a legendary immortal's residence</note>, no longer envying the flutes and songs of Zhuxi in ancient Yangzhou. <note>original: "Zhuxi" (竹西). A famous scenic spot in Yangzhou known for its musical heritage.</note>
○ <note>This symbol marks the beginning of the second stanza.</note> Wild rice and broomcorn millet are served with sweet flag; jade vessels are poured into jade-boat cups. From whose house comes the prelude to "The Water Melody"? <note>original: "Shuidiao" (水調). A popular and melancholic musical tune of the Tang and Song dynasties.</note>
The sound pierces the emerald mountains and flies away, while the evening thunder lingers.

# Gazing South of the River <gloss>original: "Wang Jiangnan" (望江南); also called "Dreaming of the South" or "Gazing at River Plum Blossoms"</gloss>

<vocab>
**The Southern Branch:** original "Nan Ke Zi" (南柯子), a specific song melody.
**Su Dongpo:** The literary name of Su Shi, a famous Song Dynasty poet.
**Gazing South of the River:** original "Wang Jiangnan" (望江南), a very popular and short lyric form.
**Lyric:** original "Ci" (詞), a genre of poetry written to match existing musical tunes.
**Level Rhyme:** original "Pingyun" (平韻), a rhyme using the 'level' tonal category.
**Rhyme Agreement:** original "Pingxie" (平叶), the requirement for a line to rhyme with the preceding level-tone rhyme.
**Yangzhou:** A major cultural and economic hub in imperial China, famous for its beauty and entertainment.
**Water Melody:** original "Shuidiao" (水調), a specific musical melody used as a template for lyrics.
</vocab>

# Lyric Poetry <gloss>original: "Ci" (詞); a genre of poetry written to specific musical tunes</gloss>

<image-desc>
A metrical diagram for the tune "Gazing South of the River" (Wang Jiangnan). Tonal symbols: White circles (○) indicate level tones, and filled dots (●) indicate oblique tones.
- Column 1: ○ ● ● (First line, three characters)
- Column 2: ● ○ ○ ● ● (Second line, five characters; starting level rhyme)
- Column 3: ● ● ● ○ ○ (Third line, five characters; level rhyme agreement)
- Column 4: ○ ○ ● ● ● ○ ○ (Fourth line, seven characters)
- Column 5: ● ● ○ ○ ● ● ● (Fifth line, seven characters; level rhyme agreement)
</image-desc>

->**Li Houzhu**<-
<note>Li Houzhu (937–978), the "Last Ruler" of the Southern Tang dynasty, is one of China’s most revered lyric poets. His work is famous for its profound sorrow, reflecting his life as a king who lost his country and became a prisoner.</note>

How much regret!
In the soul of last night's dream, 
I seemed to wander the Imperial Gardens as in days of old.
Carriages flowed like water, horses like dragons, 
The flowers and the moon caught in the height of the spring breeze.

How many tears! 
Streaked down my face and across my cheeks. 
My heart's cares must not be shared with the moon; 
The phoenix flute should not be played at the time of weeping. 
Heartbreak is now beyond any doubt. 
<note>This poem is written to the tune "Gazing South of the River" (Wang Jiangnan).</note>

# Lyric Poetry <gloss>original: "Ci" (詞); a genre of poetry written to specific musical tunes</gloss>

->**Zhu Xizhen**<-
<note>Zhu Xizhen (1081–1159), better known as Zhu Dunru, was a prominent poet of the Song Dynasty. His work often reflects a transition from youthful, carefree romanticism to a more somber, world-weary tone following the political upheavals of his era.</note>

In the courtyard of early spring, the east wind breaks at dawn.
Fine rain beats upon the mandarin ducks ○ <note>original: "Yuan yang" (鴛鴦); these birds represent devoted couples, but here they might refer to decorative carvings or actual birds huddling in the rain</note>. A sharp chill clings to the flower tips.
I look out and see the swing is still.
There is no path to go treading the green <term>Ta Qing (踏青); a traditional spring outing to enjoy the fresh grass</term> or join the game of gathering herbs <term>Dou Cao (鬪草); literally "grass combat," a folk game involving finding and comparing rare plants or interlocking grass stems to see which breaks first</term>.

○ Since we parted, news has been lost among the emerald clouds.
Facing this fine scenery, sorrows are many and joys are few.
I wait for the swallows <note>original: "Yan yu" (燕于); swallows were traditionally believed to carry letters between separated lovers</note> to bring word of you.
Even though the red apricots have bloomed, you have not yet arrived.

### Loving the Embroidered Quilt <gloss>original: "Lian Xiu Qin" (戀繡衾)</gloss>

The first stanza consists of four lines and three rhymes, totaling twenty-seven characters. The second stanza follows the same structure as the first.

<image-desc>
A prosodic diagram for the tune "Loving the Embroidered Quilt," detailing the metrical structure of the lyrics. It consists of several vertical columns of symbols: solid black dots (●) for oblique tones, open circles (○) for level tones, and vertically bisected circles (◐) indicating tonal flexibility. 

Small-character annotations are placed alongside these columns to guide the reader:
- The first column of dots is labeled "First line, seven characters, beginning with a level rhyme" <note>original: "Shou ju qi zi ping yun qi" (首句七字 平韻起)</note>.
- Subsequent columns are labeled "Second line, seven characters, matching level rhyme" <note>original: "Er ju qi zi ping xie" (二句七字 平叶)</note>, "Third line, six characters," and "Fourth line, seven characters, matching level rhyme."
- The notation continues for the second half of the stanza with labels such as "Opening line, seven characters" and "Second line, seven characters, matching level rhyme."
</image-desc>

<summary>This page presents a lyric poem by the Song dynasty poet Zhu Dunru set to the tune "Loving the Embroidered Quilt," expressing themes of spring loneliness and the wait for a distant loved one. It includes a technical metrical diagram used by scholars and poets to ensure new lyrics follow the correct tonal patterns of the melody.</summary>

# Lyric Poetry <gloss>original: "Ci" (詞)</gloss>

->**Lu Fangweng**<-
<note>Lu Fangweng is the art name of Lu You (1125–1210), one of the most prolific and beloved poets of the Southern Song Dynasty. His name "Fangweng" translates to "The Old Man who Lets Himself Go" or "The Unrestrained Old Man," reflecting his persona of a defiant patriot-scholar in retirement.</note>

I did not regret trading my sable fur coat <note>Sable fur represents high official rank</note> for a fisherman’s hut. Alas, among the people of this age, who recognizes the wisdom of the "Old Man who Lets Himself Go"? For a moment, I rely on the gentle, steady wind; a few notes of the evening bell drift from beyond the Langlin woods.

○ Do not mock the smallness of this snail-shell hut where I live in seclusion. It holds ten thousand layers of misty mountains and clouded waters. For half a lifetime, I looked upon such scenes in paintings <term>Danqing (丹青); literally "vermilion and blue-green," referring to the pigments used in traditional Chinese landscape painting</term>; I rejoice that now, I am standing within the painting itself.

# Lyric Poetry <gloss>original: "Ci" (詞)</gloss>
## Poetry Remnants <gloss>original: "Shiyu" (詩餘); a term suggesting that these lyrics were the artistic "overflow" or evolution of traditional poetry</gloss>

On the branch, a warbling oriole is heard through tears; new tear-stains mingle with old tear-stains. All spring, there has been no news from the fish or the birds <note>In Chinese lore, fish and birds are traditional metaphors for messengers or the delivery of letters</note>; a thousand miles of mountain passes weary the dreaming soul. 〇 Not a single word is spoken to the fragrant wine-cup; heartbreak is arranged until the arrival of dusk. Just as the lamp-wick is finally lit, rain strikes the pear blossoms, and the door is tightly shut.
<note>This poem is a famous example of the "Spring in the Jade Pavilion" tune, typically attributed to the statesman and poet Yan Shu (991–1055). It is a classic "boudoir lament" depicting a woman’s loneliness and the melancholy of spring.</note>

->**Lyric Poetry**<-

->**Yan Yuanxian**<-
<note>Yan Yuanxian is the posthumous name of Yan Shu (991–1055), a prominent Song Dynasty statesman and a master of the "small song" (*xiao ling*) style of lyric poetry.</note>

By green willows and fragrant grass on the road to the roadside pavilion, the young man heartlessly abandoned me, departing with such ease. At the tower’s peak, a fading dream is broken by the fifth-watch bell; beneath the flowers, the sorrow of parting mingles with the March rain. 〇 Being heartless is not as bitter as having too much feeling; a single inch of the heart turns into ten thousand tangled threads of grief. The horizon’s edge and the corners of the sea have their limit, but only the longing of love remains without an end.
<note>This poem follows the tune "Treading the Sedge" (*Ta Suo Xing*). It is one of Yan Shu's most famous works, reflecting on the permanence of longing compared to the vast but finite physical world.</note>

Slender clouds craft delicate wonders, flying stars convey a lingering regret; the Silver River <note>original: "Yin Han"; refers to the Milky Way</note> is crossed in secret, far and wide. ## Lyric Poetry

->Li Houzhu<-
<note>Li Houzhu (r. 961–975), born Li Yu, was the last ruler of the Southern Tang dynasty. After his kingdom was conquered by the Song, he lived in captivity, where he wrote his most poignant lyrics expressing grief for his lost realm.</note>

Spring flowers and the autumn moon—when will they ever end? How much of the past is known? In my small building last night, the east wind blew again; I cannot bear to look back at my lost kingdom under the bright moon. ◯ The carved railings and jade steps should still be there, but the rosy faces of the people have surely changed. If you ask me how much... <note>The text cuts off here. The famous concluding line is: "If you ask me how much sorrow one man can bear, it is just like a river of spring water flowing east."</note>

### Lyrics for the Ninth Day
<note>The "Ninth Day" refers to the Double Ninth Festival, a traditional holiday on the ninth day of the ninth lunar month, associated with climbing mountains, drinking chrysanthemum wine, and reflecting on the brevity of life.</note>

By **Su Dongpo**
<note>Su Dongpo (1037–1101) is one of the most celebrated polymaths of the Song dynasty, famous for his poetry, calligraphy, and gastronomy.</note>

The frost has fallen and the water recedes, revealing shallow, shimmering green ripples and distant sandbars. The strength of the wine gradually fades as the wind turns soft; a chilling breeze tugs at my tattered hat, which, as if out of affection, stubbornly clings to my head. <gloss>How can poetry and wine serve as a fitting response to this day?</gloss> Let us simply take up our clear goblets and see the autumn off. In the end, all worldly affairs are nothing but a dream. Let it be, let it be! By tomorrow, these "yellow flowers" <term>Yellow flowers: A poetic name for chrysanthemums, which are central to the Double Ninth Festival</term> will have faded, and even the butterflies will be left to grieve.

->Lyric: **Summer Night**<-

->By **Wang Zhuke**<-
<note>Wang Zhuke, literally "Wang the Banished Guest," is likely a pseudonym for a poet living in exile or a traveler.</note>

A hundred feet of clear spring water, fireflies flickering one after another. Reflecting an elegant air, the green firmiana trees and emerald bamboo stand tall. Facing the thousand-pace winding corridor, hidden behind layer upon layer of curtains, I lean my head against a small pillow of **cold jade**. <note>original: "寒玉" (han yu). A poetic way to describe a cool porcelain pillow used during hot summer nights.</note> $\circ$ I try unrolling the **mermaid silk** <note>original: "鮫綃" (jiao xiao). A legendary fabric woven by mermaids; here it refers to a fine silk painting or scroll.</note> to look through the entire scroll, seeing a vista of the **Xiao and Xiang rivers** <note>The Xiao-Xiang region in modern Hunan is a classic motif in Chinese art, symbolizing misty, water-bound landscapes and poetic melancholy.</note> in deep, congealed green. I wait for the **jade water-clock** <note>original: "玉漏" (yu lou). An ancient water-clock used to measure time at night.</note> to chime through the flowers, for the Milky Way to hang low to the earth, and for the moon to rise over the curve of the railing.

---

# The Soul of the Drunkard
<note>original: "Zui Luo Po." A *cipai*, or tune title. The name suggests a state of being lost in wine or a wandering spirit.</note>

The first stanza consists of five lines and four rhymes, totaling twenty-seven characters. The second stanza consists of five lines and four rhymes, totaling thirty characters.

Rhyme: thirty characters.

- Second line: Seven characters, matching the oblique rhyme.

</image-desc>

### Lyric
#### A Beauty Playing the Flute
->By Zhang Ziye<-
<note>Zhang Ziye (990–1078) is the courtesy name of Zhang Xian, a renowned poet of the Northern Song Dynasty famous for his delicate and romantic lyrics.</note>

Clouds are light and willows tender; her **palace-style hair-bun** <note>original: "內家髻子" (neijia jizi). A hairstyle fashionable within the imperial court, often imitated by high society.</note> is newly groomed. Her natural fragrance and true beauty are things others can hardly imitate. She plays the transverse flute alone as the moon pales and the sky hangs down like a heavy curtain.
○ Her vermilion lips part slightly, like the opening of a **cherry blossom bud** <note>original: "櫻桃萼" (yingtao e). A classic poetic metaphor for a woman's small, beautiful mouth.</note>. Leaning against the tower, she stands at the corner of the railing. The night is chilly, her fingers cold, and her silk garments are thin. The sound of the flute enters the frosty forest; with a rustling *su-su*, it startles the plum blossoms until they fall.

---

### Treading on the Sedge
<note>original: "踏莎行" (Ta Sha Xing). A popular melody title for lyrics; the name evokes a spring walk through the grass.</note>
The first stanza has five lines with three rhymes, totaling twenty-nine characters. The second stanza follows the same pattern.

## Lyric: An Inn in Chenzhou
<note>This poem is widely known by the tune title "Treading on the Sedge" (Tasha Xing). It was written by Qin Guan while he was exiled to the remote south.</note>

->Qin Shaoyou<-
<note>original: "泰少游" (Tai Shaoyou). Likely a transcription error for "秦少游" (Qin Shaoyou), the courtesy name of the poet Qin Guan (1049–1100).</note>

Mist shrouds the pavilions; the moon loses the ferry crossing. My gaze toward the **Peach Blossom Spring** <note>A mythical utopian land of peace; here used to represent a home or hope that is now unreachable.</note> is severed; there is no path to find. How can I endure this lonely inn, locked against the spring cold?

Amidst the cuckoo’s cries, the setting sun fades. A plum blossom is sent via the post; a letter arrives <note>original: "魚傳尺素" (yu chuan chi su). Literally "a fish carries a foot of white silk," an ancient idiom for receiving a letter.</note> from afar. This sorrow piles up, beyond all counting. The Chen River fortunately flows far away on its own; but for whom do the Chen Mountains remain, watching the river depart for the Xiao and Xiang?

---

# Lyric Poetry
One

### Palace Lyric
<note>Gong Ci: A genre of poetry focused on the lives, often melancholic, of women living within the imperial palace walls.</note>

->Wei Duanyi<-
<note>original: "韋端已" (Wei Duanyi). The courtesy name of Wei Zhuang (c. 836–910), a prominent poet known for his poignant and rhythmic style during the late Tang and Early Five Dynasties.</note>

Once the gates of Zhaoyang <note>A palace associated with the Han Empress Zhao Feiyan; used here as a general term for the residence of the Emperor's favored consorts.</note> were closed, spring has followed spring. In the cold of night, the palace water-clock <note>original: "宮漏" (gonglou). An ancient water clock used in the palace to mark the long hours of the night.</note> drips endlessly. I dream of the Emperor's favor; past events secretly consume the soul.

My silk robes are damp; my red sleeves bear the marks of tears. Singing and the sound of flutes are heard from beyond the many-layered palace gates. In the distant courtyard, the fragrant grass grows green. Leaning against the Long Gate <note>The "Long Gate Palace" (Changmen) refers to the residence where Empress Chen of the Han Dynasty was sequestered after losing the Emperor's favor; it is a classic literary symbol of abandonment.</note>, to whom can I speak of this endless melancholy? Lost in thought, I stand within the palace halls as twilight falls.

---

<note>Ye You Gong: A specific "tune title" or metrical template for Ci poetry.</note>

->First stanza: five phrases, four rhymes, twenty-nine characters. Second stanza: five phrases, four rhymes, twenty-eight characters.<-

Rhyming on the "level" or "even" tone, which produces a sustained, melodic sound.</note>, **Zhaoyang Palace**, **Long Gate Palace**, **Dreaming of the Emperor’s favor**, **Lost in thought** (Condensed emotion).

# A Bushel of Pearls
<note>Yi Hu Zhu: A "tune title" (cipai) that serves as a metrical template for lyric poetry. The name originates from a story about the Tang Emperor Xuanzong sending a bushel of pearls to his consort, Jiang Caiping (the Plum Consort).</note>

<gloss>First stanza: five phrases, four rhymes, twenty-seven characters. Second stanza: five phrases, four rhymes.</gloss>

<note>The following diagram (represented here by its descriptive notes) illustrates the required tonal patterns for this melody. Open circles indicate "Level" tones, while solid circles indicate "Oblique" tones.</note>

### Metrical Structure
**First Stanza:**
*   Phrase 1: Six characters, beginning with an oblique rhyme.
*   Phrase 2: Seven characters, rhyming in the oblique tone.
*   Phrase 3: Seven characters, rhyming in the oblique tone.
*   Phrase 4: Three characters, oblique tone.
*   Phrase 5: Four characters, rhyming in the oblique tone.

**Second Stanza:**
*   Phrase 1: Five characters, oblique tone.
*   Phrase 2: Seven characters, rhyming in the oblique tone.
*   Phrase 3: Seven characters, rhyming in the oblique tone.
*   Phrase 4: Six characters, oblique tone.
*   Phrase 5: Three characters, rhyming in the oblique tone.

***

Alone at night, the cold penetrates <note>original: "佼" (jiǎo); likely an OCR error or variant for "侵" (qīn), meaning to invade or soak through.</note> my kingfisher-green quilt. Alas, my secluded dreams <term>幽夢 (youmeng): Refers to the deep, often elusive dreams of a person in solitude.</term> will not come, and I rise once more. Wishing to write of new sorrows, my tears splash upon the paper. I remember the imperial favors I once received, and sigh that my remaining life has now come to this.

◯ The burnt embers of the lamp fall with a rustle <note>original: "蔌蔌" (sùsù). An onomatopoeia for the sound of falling petals or, in this case, the charred remains of a lamp wick, which was often read as a fortune-telling omen.</note>. I ask: at this moment, what news of that person does this omen report? Though the Long Gate <note>The Long Gate Palace (Changmen) is a classic literary symbol for the residence of a discarded or lonely imperial consort.</note> is but a few inches <note>original: "咫尺" (zhǐchǐ). Literally a very short distance; "so close yet so far."</note> away, it feels further than ten thousand miles. I hate that my Lord's heart is like a precarious railing—one cannot lean upon it for long.

->Lu Fangweng<-
<note>The pseudonym of Lu You (1125–1210), one of the most prolific and celebrated poets of the Southern Song Dynasty.</note>

<keywords>Ci poetry, A Bushel of Pearls, Lu You, Long Gate Palace, palace lament, metrical structure, Song Dynasty literature</keywords>

# Thirty Characters of Rhyme
<note>While the heading refers to the rhyming structure, the poem below follows the melody known as "A Bushel of Pearls" (Yi Hu Zhu).</note>

<image-desc>A metrical diagram for a Ci poem. It consists of ten vertical columns of circles representing tones: empty circles for level tones, filled circles for oblique tones, and half-filled circles for variable tones. Small vertical text between the columns outlines a two-stanza structure.</image-desc>

<gloss>First line: four characters, starting with an oblique rhyme.</gloss>
<gloss>Second line: seven characters, rhyming in the oblique tone.</gloss>
<gloss>Third line: seven characters, rhyming in the oblique tone.</gloss>
<gloss>Fourth line: four characters.</gloss>
<gloss>Fifth line: five characters, rhyming in the oblique tone.</gloss>
<gloss>Opening line of second stanza: seven characters, rhyming in the oblique tone.</gloss>
<gloss>Second line: seven characters, rhyming in the oblique tone.</gloss>
<gloss>Third line: seven characters, rhyming in the oblique tone.</gloss>
<gloss>Fourth line: four characters.</gloss>
<gloss>Fifth line: five characters, rhyming in the oblique tone.</gloss>

---

**Li Houzhu**
<note>original: "李後主" (Li Houzhu); refers to Li Yu (937–978), the last ruler of the Southern Tang dynasty and a foundational figure in the history of the lyric poem.</note>

Her evening makeup just finished, she lightly applies a touch of <term>Sandalwood and Aloeswood (Chen-Tan): A fragrant paste made from ground aromatic woods, used as a cosmetic or lip balm.</term>. When her mouth opens, she slightly reveals her clove-like tongue. A single clear song begins, momentarily parting her cherry-red lips. <note>Stanza marker: In the original text, this marks the transition between the two halves of the poem.</note> Within her silk sleeve, a faint deep-red stain remains; embarrassed, she quickly lets the fragrant wine <term>Stain (Wo): To soil or wet; here, she uses the wine to mask a makeup smudge or a small mistake.</term> it. Leaning sideways on the embroidered bed with effortless grace, she thoroughly chews a bit of red silk thread, then laughingly spits it toward her <term>Tan Lang: A romantic epithet for a husband or lover, originally referring to the handsome Jin dynasty poet Pan An.</term>.

<summary>This page illustrates the metrical structure and provides a famous example of the "A Bushel of Pearls" lyric form, featuring a poem by Li Yu that describes a court lady's intimate charms.</summary>
<keywords>Li Yu, Ci poetry, A Bushel of Pearls, metrical diagrams, cosmetics, Southern Tang dynasty, romantic lyrics</keywords>
